A Mysterious Person on the Chichibu Maru.
s the result of an investigation conducted by Assistant
Police Inspector Suzuki of the Water Police, a mysterious per-
son, a third class passenger on the S. S. Chichibu Maru, which
C
CS
arrived at Yokohama from Honolulu on the 2lst instant, was
(金應义)
arrested. He states that his name is Kin Ohitsu (Japanese
9
31
pronunciation of the characters), age 58, and born in Kankyo
Nando, Chosen, and that he is on his way home from Honolulu.
Upon examination of his personal effects, a watch was found in
which (the mechanical parts having been removed) 5 momme of
refined opium were concealed. Upon further examination, 1
kwamme or refined opium and 1 kwamme of crude opium were
found in his bedding and were confiscated immediately. He
olaims that he bought the opium from a certain Shin (中)
just before he left Honolulu. It appears that he intended to
sell the opium in Japan and Chosen, coming here at the in-
F
stigation of a group of opium smugglers in Honolulu. He is
APR 10 935
now being closely examined.

THEB JAPAN CRONIOLE
May 1. 1956.
15 OPIUM PEDDLERS HELD
Kereans Smuggled Drug from Saghalien
and Sold it Here
▲ gang of 15 Korean laborers that had been distribut-
ing opium throughout Japan and in Shangha1 has been ar-
rested by Tokyo police, the Miyako learned yesterday.
Their aotivities were disolosed April 6 when police
inspeeting a Korean dormitory in Minami Senji, Arakawa
Ward, prior to the visit to Tokyo of the Manohukuo Kmperor
disoovered 10 conooaled sticks of dynamite and 16 pounds
of opium. They arrested Bunsai R1, 34, in whose room the
drug was found, and Kakuta1 Chi, 33, owner of the establish-
mont.
They confessed they had been smuggling opium in from
Saghalien and peddling 1t in Tokyo, Kyoto, Nagasaki, and
other large cities of Japan as well as Shanghai and impli-
eated 13 other Koreans, who were later arrested. Popp1es
were oultivated and opium produced by a brother of one of the
gang who lives in South Saghalion.

SUBJECT:
Arrest of Opium Smugglers at Kobe.

THE HONORABLE
C)
THE SECRETARY OF STATE
WASHINGTON
SIR:
I have the honor to report that on the basis of an
artiole appearing in the YUSHIN NIPPO of January 5, 1935,
874114
a copy of a translation of which is enclosed, telling of
the arrest of seven opium smugglers in Kobe, namely,
Kiyoshi Inemura, Masaichi (or Shoichi) Yuoka, Uichi
Nakabayashi, Yoshio Fujioka, and "three others", the
Consulate requested from the local authorities informa-
tion regarding these arrests. The Consulate has now
received a report from the Governor of Hyogo-ken
transmitted through the American Embassy at Tokyo with
F
respeot to this affair. The pertinent portions of the
report are as follows:
The smugglers were Kozo Inada, aged 36, Enji Fujita,
aged 35, and Koshiro Nakamura, aged 37, Japanese subjects,
all without ocoupation. 850 momme (about 7 pounds) of
JUN 4- 1935
opium of unknown origin were imported into the port of
Otomari under the jurisdiction of the Government of
Karafuto

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Karafuto by the above-named individuals and thence
transported to Kobe. The opium was confiscated when
judgment was pronounoed by the Kobe District Court.
Inada was sentenoed on February 7, 1935 by the Kobe
District Court to six months' imprisonment. The Public
Proourator postponed prosecution in the case of Fujita
and the case of Nakamura is still being tried.
It may be noted that the report of the Governor of
Hyogo-ken does not include any of the names mentioned in
the newspaper account although the "three others" would
appear to correspond to Inada, Fujita, and Nakamura.
The Consulate is continuing its efforts to obtain
information regarding the remaining alleged smugglers
named in the newspaper article as well as the ultimate
disposition of the Nakamura case. The Department will
be kept informed in the premises.
Respectfully yours,
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
re No. 1 to despatoh No. 476 of Mr. Howard Donovan,
an Consul at Kobe, Japan, dated May 2, 1935, on the
Jot: Arrest of Opium Smugglers at Kobe.
COPY
(TRANSLATION FROM EVENING EDITION OF KOBE YUSHIN NIPPO)
January 5, 1935.
The Aioibashi Police Station, Kobe made whole-
sale arrest of smugglers of opium on December 3lst
about 10:00 P.M. at a certain assignation house at
Kitanagasa-dori, Kobe, while they were negotiating
for the sale of some 1,oo0 momme of opium (valued
at ¥5,000). The smugglers arrested were:
Kiyoshi Inamura, 42, a broker of drugs,
Otomari-cho, Karafuto;
Shoichi (or Masaichi) Yuoka, 40, a broker,
Isobe-dori 4-chome, Kobe;
Uichi Nakabayashi, 30,
Yoban-cho 2-chome, Kobe;
Yoshio Fujioka, 32,
Ishii-cho, Kobe;
and three others.
They are of a gang of smugglers consisting of a large
number of Japanese and Chinese engaged in illicit
traffic in league with a gang of smugglers in Shanghai,
and the rest of the smugglers got wind of a round-up
and covered their trails. The police is making a
nation-wide search for them, and it is reported that
the Kagacho Police Station, Yokohama has arrested
Yoshihisa Yamada, ll/3 Totsuka-cho, Tokyo, and two
others said to have been involved in the illicit
trafric. These smugglers bought a large quantity
of opium in Shanghai, and in order to blind the eyes
of the police they landed the drugs at ports in
Karafuto or Hokkaido, making a tool of seamen of
a cortain steamship company. The drugs smuggled are
estimated at more than ¥100,000.

JUN 7-1935

Japan, as far as the Embassy is aware, the Department may

Tokyo.
Excellency:
With regard to your note of March 13 suggesting
an investigation of the facts of the case involving
opium smugglers arrested by police of the Ueno station,
I have the honor to state that the request was immedi-
ately referred to the Department of Home Affairs, which
has now transmitted a report of the Superintendent of the
Metropolitan Police of which the enclosed is a copy.
The report states that the smugglers and others involved
were taken to the office of the Procurator of the Tokyo
District Court, where on March 2 charges were brought
under the provisions of the opium law. Details are con-
tained in the enclosure.
I remain, Sir,
Your obedient servant,
Kuriyama,
Chief of the Bureau of Treaties.

Domicile: No. 14,'Chuin-machi,
City of Nara, Nara prefecture.
Present address: No. 37, Nichome,
Kitahama-cho, Higashi-ku, Osaka.
(d) Teru Okamoto, head of a family, unemployed,
39 years old, born in May.
Domicile: No. 2,405, Nishisugano-machi,
Toshima-ku, Tokyo.
Present address: No. 48, Komagomehayasli-cho,
Hongo-ku, Tokyo.
(e) Kotaro Hashimoto, fourth son of Tokichi Hashimoto
who is head of a f mily and a fox-farmer by occupa-
tion, 47 years old, born in December.
Domicile: Aza Daigo, Nishinosho-mura,
Ayauta-gun, Kagawa prefecture.
Present address: No. 19, 1-chome, Higashisanjo-
minami, Honcho, Odomari-machi,
Karafuto.
(3) Facts pertaining to the offence.
(a) Kotaro Hashimoto, accused, purchased four kan and
three hundred momme of opium for eight hundred yen from a
Korean named Shu-kan-matsu (his age, address, etc. are not
known) at Toyohara, Karafuto, about August, 1934. He then
came to Tokyo carrying with him the opium purchased and
asked Teru Okamoto to dispose of the same, which was handed
over to her. Teru Okamoto, keeping part of the opium in
her house, put the remainder of the opium in a black trunk
and handed it over to Gentaro Nakai who next handed it over
to Tokuichi Sakamoto who finally handed it over to Tsunekichi
Fukui, each in turn being requested to dispose of it.
(b)

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
(b) Because Gentaro Nakai did not give them money
accruing from the sale of the opium, both Teru Okamoto
and Kotaro Hashimoto pressed Nakai for payment. In the
beginning of October of the same year, Gentaro Nakai
returned the black trunk to Teru Okamoto and Kotaro
Hashimoto, saying that the contents therein were the
same opium which he had received previously for sale.
Teru Okamoto and Kotaro Hashimoto found upon examination
that the contents were not the sane opium which they asked
him previously to sell, and that the quantity was also
reduced by about one-half. (The examination of the con-
tents by the Sanitary Burcau laboratory shovs that the same
are not opium).
(c) Teru Okamoto and Kotoro Hashimoto allege that
Gentaro Nakai has isposed of the opium whic! the asked
him to sell and has retained the money accrued from the
sale of opium, and that he returned them a quantity
of imitation opiun. Genturo Nakai denies their allega-
tions.
(d) Results of the offence.
The five persons above mentioned were escorted to
the Procurator's office on February 13 on a charge of
having violated article 3 of the opium law.
Remarks.
More than two hundred momme of opium which an officer
of the Ueno police station discovered at Teru Okamoto's
are ascertained to be genuine opium, upon examination by
the Sanitary Bureau laboratory. The quantity of opium
above refarred to is part of four kan and three hundred
momme

- 4 -
momme of opium which Teru Okamoto was asked by Kotaro
Hashimoto to sell, wnich was kept in her house.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's tele-
graphic instruction No. 60, April 10, 3 p.m., in regard
to a newspaper report to the effect that a Korean had
been arrested in Yokohama on a charge of smuggling opium,
which he had obtained in Honolulu, into Japan. The
F
Department instructed the Embassy to request full in-
formation in regard to the case from the Japanese author-
ities, in order that investigation in Honolulu might be
facilitated.
In accordance with the Department's instructions,a
request for information was promptly conveyed to the ap-
propriate

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
propriate Japanese authorities, and the Embassy has now
received through the Foreign Office a copy of a report
(undated) from the Governor of Kanagawa Prefecture to the
Sanitary Bureau of the Department of Home Afrairs covering
1/
the case. A translation of the report is enclosed herewith.
The only information in the report which it appears might
be useful to the American authorities in investicatin: the
case is that the opium in the. possession of the .oreen was
obtained from a Chinese named "Shen" in Honolulu.
Any further information received in regard to this
case will be promptly transmitted to the Department.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Facts of the case:
A Korean named Kin oh Hitsui 全 應 ) while
living in Hawaii, had been accustomed to smoke and chew
opium at a house occupied by some Chinese. On the eve of
twenty grams of liquid opium, one ram of lump opium, and

use. He smoked and chewed the opium in his own room on
board the steamer CHICHIEU IARU. He was escorted to the

Mr. Director,

Department of Home Affairs.
Report concerning opium smuggling
in connection with an arrest by the
Yokohama Water Police.
Attached herewith please find a report on the subject
above-mentioned.
Governor,
Kanagawa Prefecture.
Kin-o-hitsu
(余應
Ly ), 58 years of age,
Domicile: No. 23, Nakazato-ichido,
Gensan, Kankyo-nando,
Chosen.
Former address: 53 Parama (?) Street, Honolulu, Hawaii.
(1)
Since about November, 1929, Kin-o-hitsu was in the
habit of smoking and chewing opium twice each month at the
house occupied by a Chinese named Shen (φ) (his name is not
clearly known and he is about 60 years old) at No. 29, Pahowa (?)
Street, Honolulu. Shortly before his departure from Honolulu
for home, namely ab about 11 a.m. on March 9, 1935, Kin-o-hitsu
purchased from the Chinese Shen about twenty grams of refined
liquid opium and about one gram of seven refined solid pieces
2
of opium for $23 American currency, as well as about 218
grams

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
grams of crude opium for $1 American currency, a total of 24
for the three items above stated, for his own use and concealed
the same in his trunk. On March 1: he left Honolulu for home
on the Nippon Yusen Kaisha steamer CHICHIBU MARU. During
the period from March 13 to March 21, the day on which the
steamer arrived at Yokohama, he swallowed about ten grams
ofrefined liquid opium altogether instead of smoking in his
own steerage room at the stern, at 6 a.m., 5 p.m. and 8 p.m.
eveyy day, namely 27 times in all during the period. In
addition, on March 14 and 15 he also ate about 0.6 gram of
refined solid opium, in four pieces, instead of smoking in
forenoon and afternoon each day, namely twice daily, a total
of four time during two days.
(2)
At about 8 p.m. on March 21, the day on which he ar-
rived at Yokohama as a steerage passenger on the CHICHIBU MARU
which left Honolulu on March 12, 1935, he put in an oblong
receptacle of square section about ten grams of the refined
liquid opium which he had purchased from the Chinese Shen
above-mentioned, and concealed the same in his belly-band. He
further concealed three pieces of refined. solid opium (about
0.4 gram) in a watch on the right side of his underwear, while
about 218 grams of crude opium were wrapped in foreign paper
and put in a white cotton bag and then concealed in a straw
quiIt beneath his bedstead in his own room.
(3)
On March 21, 1935, the day on which he arrived at Yoko-
hama, he loaned $2 American currency to a Korean named Sho-
ji-en (詳時送 ), 63 years old, of No. 28, Parama '?) Street,
Honolulu, and in return he obtained one opium-smoking pot
made

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
made of iron which is an apparatus for smoking opium, and
which he wrapped in foreign paper and put in a white cotton
bag and then concealed in a straw quilt beneath his bedstead
in his room.
(4)
Kin-o-hitsu went to Hawaii about November, 1902,
and has since worked in farm yards as a laborer. On July 29,
1929, he was discharged on account of illness (beriberi),
and has since been recuperating. According to a statement
of a physician of the Century (?) Hospital on Palama (?)
Street, Honolulu, helearned that there is no proper method
but to smoke and eat opium in curing his illness and has
since smoked opium continuously.
On April 18 Kin-o-hitsu was escorted to the Public Pro-
curator's Office of Kanagawa Prefecture charged with smoking
opium and possessing opium and, utensils for smoking opium.
After examination he was set free, no judicial procedure being
taken against him.

American Embassy
Tokio, Japan
Gentlemen:
We understand that the Japanese Government
is at present very much interested in tackling the
"Opium" problem and especially the treatment of drug
addiction in hospitals.
We are just introducing in several foreign
countries the new drug "Rossium", the only specific
to treat Durg Addiction successfully. Rossium is
already being used in a Chinese Government hospital
with success.
We shall appreciate your advice with whom we
have to get in touch in order to make similar ar-
rangements with some Japanese Government hospitals.
We are confident that we can be of real service in
this matter, and thank you in advnce for the courtesy
of your advice.
Maybe you will decide to submit this matter direct-
ly to the proper Japanese authorities.
Very truly yours,
MEDICO CHEMICAL CORPORATION OF AMERICA

AMERICAN CONSULATE,

Kobe, Japan, October
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
TE
uBject: Control of Smuggling at the Port of
Kobe, Japan.
S
C
H
6
For Disrbution Check
Ye1
E
OND
Grade
For
V(
LuUSA
DEPAR
HO
LOTHE SECRETARY OF
STATE,
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
instruction of September 18, 1935, concerning the
3
measures taken at this port to prevent smuggling, and
6
894114
to report as follows:
Control over Incoming Vessels.
4 NARCOTICS/202
Passenger vessels arriving in the Port of Kobe are
ED
boarded by custom inspectors either when they dock
1935
alongside the quay or anchor at a buoy in the roadstead.
Declarations of passengers are immediately examined to
determine the possibility of prohibited articles in
baggage. The actual examination of the baggage then
takes place at several outlets of the customs compound,
the area to which foreign trade and traffic are restricted.
In comparison with examinations at other ports throughout
M
the world the baggage search at Kobe may be said to be
quite careful. Personal searcn on the other hand is
limited, and inquiries do not exoeed those concerning
the

=== Page 78 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
the presence of alcohol or tobacco on the person. Under
ordinary circumstances it would be perfectly possible
for someone dressed moderately well to pass through the
oustoms barrier, his pockets filled with narcotics. The
oriminal section of the Hyogo Prefectural Office is known
to maintain a fairly complete file of persons engaged in
the drug traffic, and in co-operation with the Water Police
who board the ship at Wada Misaki, the eastern extremity
of the harbor, the Customs officials would undertake a
rigorous search of baggege as well as person of any one
suspected of smuggling narcotics into Japan.
Technically the cargo manifest is not exanined until
it has been deposited at the Custons house with the rest
of the ship's papers, but actually in most instances a
copy is presented to the boarding customs officers when
the ship docks or anchors in the roadstead. Cargo boats
carrying no passengers turn their manifest in with the
other papers so that no previous check is possible.
Examination of cargo for narcotics ic strict, particularly
on goods arriving from China or other opium producing
areas. As goods may be kept in bond within the customs
compound it is possible that cargo containing narcotics
could be unloaded at Kobe from an arriving vessel, and
kept in bond here for later distribution. It is possible
that little attention to such cargo would be paid by the
oustoms authorities nor would they be urged by the
Prefectural police in this regard as there would be
little chance of the narcotics ultimately finding their
way into Japan. The reason for this seeming indifference
will become more apparent in later pages of this report.
Control

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Control over Vessels in the Harbor.
Little control is exercised over passenger vessels
tied alongside a quay. The loading of ships' provisions
as well as any article to be given to a member of the
orew or passenger is technically under the supervision
of a customs inspector assigned to the wharf or several
wharves as the case may be. In actual practice individuals
are perfectly free, subject to the permission of the
steamship owners, to bring articles aboard a vessel while
it is tied alongside.
Control over freight vessels carrying a few passengers
or purely cargo vessels is of somewhat more positive nature,
largely because of the character of the vessel. The customs
inspector on duty would have a good knowledge of the cargo
or other articles placed on the vessel while she was tied
to the quay. Moreover, visits to this type of vessel can
be more closely watched by the customs examiner at the
outlets to the customs compound, and frequent calls of
individuals to a certain vessel while she was in port
would undoubtedly be within the knowledge of such official.
Provisioners and ship servicers, likely contacts for
amugglers, must receive the permission indirectly, of
the customs authorities, directly, of the Harbor master
who acts under the former.
In like manner freighters carrying few passengers
and purely cargo vessels anchored in the roadsteads are
by the very nature of the position in which they are
placed subjected to a closer supervision than those
freighters tied alongside the dock or passenger vessels.
As in the other cases provisioners can not service
a

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
a vessel without permission. The visit of any craft
to a vessel in port would certainly be within the
knowledge of the customs officials. On occasion raids
are undertaken when it is believed narcotics are being
amuggled ashore. The manner in which these raids are
conducted can lead to but one conclusion that the
authorities are anxious indeed to prevent the smucgling
of opium and other narcotics into Japan.
While there are no cases on record at this Consulate
in which raids were conducted upon vessels believed to be
engaged in the opium trade in which the drug was intended
for some place other than Japan, it may be argued from
the type of control that the officials must know of many
of these cases.
Control of Departing Vessels.
Coutrol over departing vessels is only nominal.
Ships leaving the port of Kobe must receive customs
olearance. The character of the cargo loaded at Kobe
as well as that picked up at another port is known quite
thoroughly by the customs authorities. Cargo likely to
contain hidden quantities of narcotics might nave in many
instances been discovered before the vessel clears. A
careful study reveals that no effort is made to stop the
clearance of a vessel because she has narcotics aboard
unless the vessel is destined for another Japanese port
in which case there would be a likelihood of the drugs
being smuggled into Japan. In the latter case the customs
authorities, acting upon the advice of the Prefectural
police, or with their understanding, would spare no effort
to see that the narootics were confiscated and the guilty
parties

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
parties brought to justice before the vessel cleared.
A strong factor contributing to the conclusion that the
oustoms authorities must have occasional knowledge of
narcotics being placed in cargoes originating in Japan
is the now wide-spread institution called the export
guild. A large part of Japanese exports are now handled
by these guilds, and every shipment requires an examina-
tion and the stamp of the Imperial Customs. The junior
officials are in an excellent position to know if firms
belonging to these guilds are engaged in the drug traffic
with foreign countries. The question of bribery of these
officials is touched upon in the sunmary of this report.
Summary.
In conclusion it may be stated that the supervision
exercised over vessels touching at the port of Kobe is
of a character amply sufficient to ensure the control of
the drug traffic. Officialdom must have a fairly complete
knowledge of drug movements in and out of Japan. Accord-
ing to the law, imports and exports of opium and other
narcotics must have the permission of the government.
The assumption is made with respect to the exports that
none exist except those officially sanctioned. While
the Consulate at Kobe no longer deals with local officials
on matters pertaining directly to the drug traffic, it may
be stated from past experience that local officials,
whether they be customs authorities or police, exhibit
no interest in the suppression of international drug
traffic that does not involve Japan as the receiver of
those drugs. Formerly when these matters were taken up
with the prefectural police, results were obtained only
1n

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
in the cases in which drugs were being imported into
Japan. Moreover, while it is an established fact that
the police authorities maintain a blacklist of suspected
traffiokers in narcotic drugs, and the customs authorities
may be apprised of international drug traffickers
contacting Japanese producers or simply passing through
the country, the attitude is one of complete indifference.
Rather it would seem from cases on record that inter-
national drug traffickers known to the police as possible
exporters of Japanese drugs are accorded better treatment
than the ordinary law-abiding visitor to Japan. There
are instances in which junior customs examiners who there
is reason to believe had complete knowledge of the business
in which the traveler was engaged have made but a cursory
examination of the luggage, seemingly on the understanding
that the individual was not engaged in smuggling drugs
into Japan. The suspicion has reached such an extent that
this Consulate in its efforts on behalf of the Treasury
Attache stationed here to watch the whereabouts of suspected
traffickers in narcotic drugs does not in its approaches
to the local police reveal the reason for the request.
The result has been that a willingness to cooperate has
sprung up that did not formerly exist in these matters.
It might be gathered from the foregoing that Japanese
officialdom may actually be a party to the encouragement
of the drug traffic. It is believed, however, that this
is not the case, and that the real explanation lies in
the deep-seated feeling of the Japanese that they must
not permit an outside authority to have anything to do
with matters which they plainly regard as purely Japanese.
They

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
They feel that if they wish they can easily suppress
the traffic in drugs.
There is the grave possibility that junior officials
in the customs as well as ordinary police officials may
be bribed by drug smugglers. Salaries of civil officials
in Japan are notoriously low, and bribery has never been
looked upon with the same disdain as in Occidental
countries. A case is now pending in which a procurator
has been charged with accepting bribes fron a number of
narcotic smugglers.
In the apprehension and punishment of dealers in the
drug traffic the same distinction is shown, that is, the
one between importers of drugs into Japan, and exporters
alone. Time and again sentences in the latter cases will
be commuted or be ridiculously light. The above conclusions
apply equally well to the Port of Osaka, also located in
this consular district.
Respatfully yours,
Kenneth C. Krentz,
American Consul.

Ships entering the port of Yokohema are searched
203
by the Yokohama Water Police principally for the pur-
pose of ascertaining if they carry on board undesireable
characters detrimental to public morels and by quarantine
doctors for communicable diseases.
A search for con-
M
traband articles is not an ordinary practice. Within
the past

the past few years the only time when a diligent search
of all ships arriving at the port was carried cut was
during that period immediately before the visit of the
Emperor of "Manchukuo" in April of 1935. The search
was for persons believed to hold intentions against the
life of the Emperor and for concealed lethal weapons.
The majority of ships calling at Yokchama are
moored to buoys either in the inner or outer herbors
and cargo is discherged into lichters. As far as can
be ascertained there are no precautions taen to pre-
vent the smuggling of narcotics into the country from
ships so berthed.
Launehes serving vessels moored in
the harpor are licensed for service only witnin the
limits of Yokohama Harbor, out there is no reliable
check upon their activities, especially under cover of
darkness. The approach to Yokonume Haroor throuch
Tokyo Bay is dotted at all times with small fisiine
craft and there would appear to pe no reliaple check
upon any extraneous ect vities in wnich they micht easily
engage.
There would eppear to be no serious handicap to
the smuggling of narcotics from the city of Yokohama
to ships outward bound from the port. At the present
time a representative of the Imperial Customs checks
outward cargo manifests aboard the exporting vessel,
but this check is aimed principally at products affected
by export quota restrictions.
Insofar as the smuggling of narcotics is con-
cerned the same opportunities are present for smuggling
out of the country as are present for smuggling into
Japan. Launches serving vessels in the harbor are
subject

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
subjoct to little or no restriction. Persons going to
vessels moored to buoys in the huroor are . ubject to

and residents of Yokoheme, pass ngers, and crew members
are subject to no restrictions in boerdin these vessels.
Cargo mey be transshi pea fron vessel to vessel within
the Customs Enclosure without restriction.
The city of Yokohema is, it is pelieved, the last
important port in the Far ast where peadlers of sundry
merchandise are allowed ,poard ships enterin: the naroor.
These peddlers usua ly frequent the pessencer vessels,
but are at times found o: fering their wares on frei nters.
At Yokohema American vessuls re ore urfected uy these
peddlers than shiis of other n ticn: lities. The peudlers
have found thet pass ngers on Americen vessels spend money
more freely and that crew members of merican vessels
have more money for sundry purchases than crew members
of other vessels.
These peddlers have organized associntions and des-
pite the efforts of steamship compunies to prohibit their
boarding vessels they are still permitted to do so due
to what would eppear to be connivance on the part of Yoko-
hama officials.
Nominally the peddlers are subject to search upon
boarding any vessel, but it has been proved, in the case
of the Dollar Steamship Lines at least, that the watch-
men employed by the steamship companies are in the em-
ploy of the peddlers associations. Efficient watchmen
cannot

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
be employed at a rate of wages commensurate with the
duty involved who will uct in accordance with their
instructions - to search every uunule carried avoard
a vessel - because of fear of physical violence ut the
hands members of the peddlers associations or ruffians
in the employ of the associations.
In th aut mn of 19z6 the American agent of the
Dollar Steamship Lines was seriously beaten by members
of a peddlers' association because a short time before
he had issued instructions that no peadlers were to be
allowed aboard any of the vessels of his line. since
that time there has been a continuous squaoble between
the local office of the Do ler Steamship Lines and the
associations concerning the number of association members
who would be allowed sboard eaci ship. At present the
local egent of the Dollar Steamship Lines has reached a
compromise agreement with the associations whereby a
limited number of permits to boerd each ship is issued
to the associetions. The locel agent in view of local
conditions hesitates to prohibit the peddlers from
boarding the vessels of is line, in spite of a mandatory
ruling from the head office of the Dollar Steamship Lines
in the Far East at Shanghai, because of fear of physical
violence to himself and his family and to foreign members
of his staff and their families.
It is known that peddlers habitually smuggle cheap
whiskey of native manufacture aboard vessels to members
of the crew. The smuggling of this particular article
is effected by concealment in bundles of merchandise
brought aboard ostensibly for sale and is made possible
by the lack of control over casual visitors to the Customs
Enclosure

Enolosure and culpable negligence on the part of watch-
men employed by steamship companies, who, as has already
been stated,are more of ten than not in the employ of
peddlers' associations.
Within the past few months the local agent of the
Dollar Steamship Lines has endeavored to gain permission
to place gangs of stevedores aboard his vessels at
quarantine in order to heve hatches removed and ship's
gear ready to discharge or load cargo immediately the
ship came alongside the pier. This request has been
refused repeatedly. Recently, however, he received a
personal call from the Superintendent of the Yokohama
Water Police Station who steted that he was calling in
a purely personal capacity as a friend of the president
of one of the largest peddlers' associations - a retired
superintendent of a locel police station - and suggested
that in return for a larger number of permits to board
vessels issued to the particular association he was
willing to "reconsider" the refusal of the Water Police
to allow gangs of stevodores to board vessels at
quarantine. This situation is of recent origin and a
decision on the part of the local office of the Dollar
Steamship Company is still pending. The local agent does
not wish to extend further priveliges to the peddlers,
but at the same time, in view of the source of the
"auggestion", he stands in fear of reprisals from the
Water Police officials in the event thet he does not
oomply with it.
Ship's stores taken aboard at Yokohama are checked

Lewis travelled to the United States aboard the M.V.

cisco, Schwartz in Yokohama is said to have received a
telegram from his wife, who was then in San Francisco,
to the effect that "landing was impossible". Lewis has
since returned to the Far East and is at present in
Shanghai where he is said to be engageu in the pro-
motion of prize fights.
Lewis was in Yokohama during the latter part of
1935 and on August 10, 1935 executed at this consulate
an afridavit conoerning the loss of his passport. At

relief to this office and to the American Association
of Yokohama. In view of information received from the
American Consulate General at Shanghai assistance was
refuseu. On September 4, l935 Lewis sailed for the
United States aboard the S.S. "Wales Maru", his passage

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.98)
LEMORANDUM BY THE CONSULATE GENERAL, TOKYO,
TO ACCOMPANY CONFIDENTIAL DESPATCH NO. 221,
DATED JUNE 12, 1936, FROM THE AMERICAN
CONSULATE, YOKOHAMA.
The subject of the above-mentioned despatch, Max
Sohwartz, is known to the Consulate General through
frequent calls at this office with regard to the afore-
mentioned photographic enterprise whioh Schwartz and
his associates have finally succeeded in establishing.
Sohwartz originally called at the Consulate General
in the early part or March, l936, to request advice as
to the possibility of establishing a photographic
enterprise. He was advised to make application for a
license to the Ministry of Commerce and Industry and
also to martial law headquarters in Tokyo.(Tokyo at
that time was under martial law and at the date of his
call the February 26th uprising and incident had just
been liquidated.)
Consul Hutchinson informally suggested to Schwartz
that it would be very difficult for him to secure per-
mission to engage in such work. On March tenth, Schwartz
again called and stated that his application had been
refused by the Japanese authorities. He then suggested
the possibility of act ing through a Japanese, who was
to secure the license and would be dummy head of the
firm. He requested information as to whether they
would be able to secure protection from the American
Government in the event that the deception should be
discovered or that the Japanese at the head of the firm
should "doubleeross" them. He was informally advised
that under the circumstances the Consulate General did
not see how protection could be effectively or properly
furnished to him. He, however, proceeded with his
plans and even appeared at the Consulate General with
one Mr. Yokoyama who had secured a license for the
business, and requested that the contract between Yoko-
yama and Schwartz be witnessed by this office. Inas-
much as this was a notarial service, the Consulate
General felt it incumbent to perform the desired
service. Every means, however, was used to dissuade
him from his undertaking. It was pointed out to him
that he would be in serious trouble with the gendarmerie
and martial law headquarters if the deception was dis-
covered.
It is interesting to note that repeated question-
ing did not elicit any clear information as to just
what the "photographic enterprise" was to be.
Aelnied
American Consul.
merioan Consulate General,
Tokyo, Japan,
June 13, 1936.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
AMERICAN EMBASSY
Tokyo, July 22, 1936.
No. 1950.
SUBJECT: FOREIGN OFFICE ORDINANCES FOR THE
CONTROL OF JAPANESE NARCOTICS TRAFFICKERS
IN CHINA AND MANCHURIA.
3
August 18 1956
C:1
111
13 01 92
894.I14-NARCOTICS/2
101
of
C
FARFASTERNARS
(1)
cC
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
2
a
9
I have the honor to report that the OFFICIAL GAZETTE
894.114)
or the Japanese Government on June 30, 1936, published the
texts of three Foreign Office Ordinances, promulgated that
day, for the control of narcotics traffickers of Japanese
nationality in China and Manchuria. Translations of these
ordinances have been made by the Embassy and are enclosed
1/2/3
herewith. The ordinances are for the guidance and use of
FILED
C
the Japanese diplomatic and consular officers stationed in
91936
China and Manohuria and apply only to those persons over
whom suoh officers erercise ertraterritorial jurisdiction.
The word "person", therefore, in the ordinances, should be
understood to mean a person of Japanese nationality.
It

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
7.2
- 2 -
It will be observed that the ordinances impose, for
infringements of the regulations therein set forth, only the
usual Japanese penalties of three months' imprisonment or
fines of one hundred yen. It appears that no heavier
penalties could be imposed because the ordinances are based
upon the Japanese law, which imposes only those penalties.
The Chief of the Bureau of Treaties of the Japanese
Foreign Office explained to a member of the staff of the
Embassy that there had previously been an ordinance for
the control of Japanese traffickers in opium and narcotic
drugs in China, but that both opium and manufactured drugs
were included in the same ordinance. As the list of
varieties of manufactured drugs had grown larger in recent
years, it was decided to divide the ordinance, drafting one
to cover raw opium, smoking opium and utensils for opium
smoking, and another to cover all the modern varieties of
narootic drugs. The ordinance covering the traffic in
manufactured narcotic drugs in Manchuria, it was explained,
was issued because of the recent partial relinquishment of
ertraterritoriality in "Manchukuo" by Japan. Formerly the
persons of Japanese nationality in "Manchukuo" were con-
sidered to be controlled by the same regulations as those
governing persons living under Japanese extraterritorial
jurisdiction in China. They were therefore controlled by
the old ordinance concerning the trade in opium and nar-
cotic drugs in China. With the partial relinquishment of
extraterritorial

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
1-3
- 3 -
extraterritorial rights, however, it became necessary
to issue a special ordinance to control traffickers of
Japanese nationality in manufactured narcotic drugs in
"Manchukuo". The "Manchukuo" law, it was explained,
covers only the traffic in raw and prepared opium (which
is controlled by the monopoly law) and does not cover the
traffic in manufactured drugs, therefore rendering it
necessary for the Japanese Government to take this step
in order that control might be exercised over Japanese
traffickers in such drugs.
The Chief of the Bureau of Treaties also stated that,
with the new ordinances, instructions were sent to Japanese
diplomatic and consular officers in China and Manchuria to
exercise stricter control in the future over traffickers
of Japanese n ionality in opium and manufactured narcotic
drugs.
Respectfully yours,
一人
Joseph C. Grew

Control of Narcotics in Manchukuo.

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No.1950 of July 22, 1936, from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
Foreign Office Ordinance
No. 10 (Publ1shed in OFFICIAL GAZETTE
June 30, 1936)
Ordinance relating to the Control
of Opium in the Republic of China.
The ordinance relating to the control of opium
in the Republic of China is as follows:
June 30, 1936.
Hachiro Arita,
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Ordinance No. l0 Relating to the Control of Opium in China.
Article 1. The cultivation of poppy for the produc-
tion of opium is prohibited.
Article 2. The importation into China and the exporta-
tion from China of refined opium or utensils for the smok-
ing of opium are prohibited.
Article 3. The importation into China and the exporta-
tion from China of raw opium, its manufacture, sale, pur-
chase, possession and transportation, and to give or re-
ceive raw opium, are prohibited.
Artiole 4. A person who has violated any prohibitions
of Articles 1 to 3 shall be sentenced to penal servitude
not exceeding three months or to a fine not exceeding one
hundred yen. A person who attempts to violate the prohibi-
tions of Articles 1 to 3 shall be properly punished.
Article 5. A person who has instigated or assisted a
person who is not a Japanese subject to violate the prohi-
bitions contained in Articles 1 to 3 shall be sentenced to
penal servitude not longer than three months or a fine not
oxceeding one hundred yen.
Article 6. Such articles as may be used in connection
with violations of Articles 1 to 3 may be detained or con-
fiscated by administrative disposition, ercepting such arti-
oles as are confiscated by judicial procedure.
Article 7. When the representatives of a juridical
person or his employees or other persons engaged violate
the provisions of this ordinance the penal clauses of this
ordinance shall apply to the said representatives of the
juridical person.
Additional Rules:
This ordinance takes effect from the lst of July, 1936.
The Ordinance relating to the cotrol of Opium and
Narcotios

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.92)
- 2 -
Narcotics in China promulgated as Foreign Office Ordinance
No. 8 of 1928 ceasos to be effective on the day when
this ordinance takes effect.

Foreign Ofrice Ordinance No. 11
(Published in OFFICIAL GAZETTE

Ordinance relating to the Control
of Narcotics in the Republic of China.
The ordinance relating to the control of narcotics in
the Republic of China is as follows:
June 30, 1936.
Hachiro Arita,
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Ordinance No. 1l relating to the Control
of Narcotics in China.
Article 1. The term "narcotics" as used in this
Article includes the following:
(1) Morphine and diacethyl morphine, other mor-
phine ester and salts thereof;
(2) Crude morphine, coca leaves and crude cocaine;
(3) Ecgonine and cocaine, other ecgonine ester and
salts thereof;
(4) Dihydro oxycodeinon, dihydro codoinon, dihydro
morphinen, acethyl dihydro codeinon, dihydro
morphine and their esters and salts;
(5) Codoine, ethyl morphine, benzyl morphine, other
morphine ether and salts thereof;
(6) Morphine N. Oxyed, other nitrogenous morphine
and derivatives thereof;
(7) Dihydro codeine and thebaine and salts thereof;
(8) Those in which more than 2/1000 of morphine,
morphine ester (excepting diacethyl morphine), or
morphine ether (excepting codeine and ethyl mor-
phine), or diacethyl morphine are detected as the
result of chemical analysis;
(9) Substances containing more than 2/1000 of dihydro
oxicodeine, dihydro codeinon, dihydro morphinon,
acethyl dihydro codeinon, dihydro morphine, or
their ewter, or nitrogenous morphine or deriva-
tives thereof are detected as the result of chemi-
cal analysis;
(10) Substances containing more than 1/1000 of ecgonine
or cocaine or other ecgonine ester are detected
as the result of chemical analysis;
(11)

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(11) Indian hemp, gum resins thereof and substances
containing gum resins of Indian hemp;
(12) Opium for medical purposes;
(13) Articles dosignated by the Minister for Foreign
Affairs.
Article 2. The term "export" and "import" used in
this Ordinance mean respectively "export from China" and
"import to China".
Article 3. Narcotics cannot be manufactured or ex-
ported.
Article 4. Except for medical purposes, the use of
narcotics for one's self or for another is prohibited.
Article 5. Except as provided in this Ordinance,
the importation, sale, purchase, giving, receiving, posession,
and owning for the purpose of sale, of narcotics, are
prohibited.
Article 6. In caae medical practitioners, dentists,
veterinary surgeons, pharmaceutists, or druggists, or
hospitals or schools require narootics for professional
purposes or for the purpose of scientific research they
may import them wit! the permission of the Imperial Japanese
consular authorities concerned (of the dis ricts in which
the applicants reside) in accordance with the pertinent
regulations or rules of the Republic of China which have
been recognized by the Imperial Japanese Government.
Article 7. A person desiring to import narcotics in
accordance with the provisions of the preceding Article
shall apply in writing to the Imperial Japanese consular
authorities of the place wherein he resides, mentioning
the following matters:
(1) Names of articles and quantity thereof;
(2) Object of importation;
(3) Name of consigner (in case of a juridical person
the name of that juridical person), and business
address;
(4) Period of importation;
(5) Route or manner of transportation;
(6) The place of import.
If, after receiving a permit, the person thus authorized
desires to alter one or other of the specifications mentioned
in (1) to (6) he shall obtain new permission as to the items
which he is desirous of changing.
When

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
When the authorities concerned have given permission
on the import of narcotics as mentioned in Paragraph 1 of
the preceding Article they shall issue to the applioant an
import permit and a certiricate attesting to the permission
of import.
Article 8. A person who has imported narootics with
the permission of the authorities concerned as mentioned
in the preceding Article shall report the fact to the Im-
perial Japanese consular authorities, producing at the same
time the copies of the import permit and the certificate
attesting to the permit of import affixed to the artiole
imported, or copy of any certificate attesting to change
of the destination.
In case the person who has obtained the permission
mentioned in the preceding Article has not imported the
article he shall return the import permit and the certifi-
cate attesting to the permission of import to the Imperial
Japanose consular authorities of the place in which he re-
sides, within ten days from the day when the period of im-
port expired.
A person who has carried or taken delivery of narcotics
from the places outside of the jurisdiction of the Imperial
Japanese consulate, or transported them to a place outside
the jurisdiction of the Imperial Japanese consulate, shall
report the fact within ten days to the Imperial Japanese
consular authorities of the place wherein he resides.
Article 9. Pharmaceutists or druggists cannot sell
or otherwise hand over narcotics unless they receive from
medical practitioners, dentists, veterinary surgeons, phar-
maceutists, druggists, or hospitals, schools or officially
recognized foreign medical practitioners, dentists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists, or druggists bills of sale
certified by the Imperial Japanese consular authorities.
This does not, however, apply when pharmaceutists sell or dis-
pense narcotics in accordance with prescriptions issued by
medical practitioners, dentists, or veterinary surgeons, or
officially recognized foreign medical practitioners, dentists
or veterinary surgeons.
In the bills of sale mentioned in the foregoing para-
graph there shall be included the name of the narcotics,
quantity thereof, object of use, date of purchase, place
of business of the purchaser, and purchaser's occupation and
name, and the seal of the purchaser shall be put to the
same.
In receiving or purchasing narcotics from officially
recognized foreign pharmaceutists or druggists the regula-
tions of Paragraph 1 shall equally apply.
The bills of sale or presoriptions shall be kept for
three

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
three years from the date of their issuance.
Article 10. In case medical practitioners, dentists,
veterinary surgeons, pharmaceutists or drugists who dis-
continue their occupation or withdraw from the Republio
of China have in their possession unused narootics, they
shall speedily transfer such narcotics to other medical
practitioners, dentists, veterinary surgeons, pharmaceutists
or druggists who are Japanese subjects.
In the aforementioned case, the transferer shall pre-
sent to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities a writ-
ten report mentioning therein the name of articles the
narcotics transferred, quantity thereof, place of business,
and date of transfer, such statement to be jointly signed
by the transferer and the transferee, with their seals being
put to the document.
In case such narcotics cannot be transferred according
to the provisions of Paragraph 1 of this Article the matter
shall be reported to the Imperial Japanese consular authori-
ties of the plaoe where the transferer resides and be sub-
ject to the direction of the authorities concerned.
Article ll. In case narcotics have been rendered use-
less on account of alteration or decomposition or on some
other account, medical practitioners, dentists, veterinary
surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists shall report the matter
in writing to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities of
the place they reside in, mentioning therein the name of the
narcotics and quantity thereof, and producing at the same
time such narcotics.
In case a medical practitioner or a dentist, or a veteri-
nary surgeon, or a pharmaceutist or a druggist dies or be-
comes missing, whoever learns that the deceased or missing
medical practitioner or other person was in possession of a
stock of unused narcotics shall at once report the matter
to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities of the place
in which the deceased or missing permon had resided.
As to the disposition of the narcotics mentioned in the
two preceding paragraphs the matter shall be subject to the
direction of the Imperial Japanese consular authorities
concerned.
Article 12. Modical practitioners, dentists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists shall distinguish
narcotics from other medicines or drugs and keep them in a
locked place.
Druggists cannot sell, distribute, or give narcotics in
small portions.
Article 13. Medical practitioners, dontists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists shall keep fired
books

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
books in which they shall enter the amount or quantity,
etc., of consumption of narcotics, their sale and purchase,
transfer, etc., and keep such records for three years from
the date of their entry. Besides, they shull make a table
showing the amount, etc., of narcotics purchased and sold
or received and transfarred during each year and submit
the table to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities
concerned by the end of January of the following year.
The Imperial Japanese consular authorities ooncerned
may examine the books mentioned in the preceding paragraph,
or the narcotics kept by those mentioned in the preceding
paragraph.
Article 14, The Imperial Japanese consular authorities
may, in connection with the booksmentioned in the preceding
Article and other matters, issue to the nersons interested
such orders as deemed necessary for control.
Article 15. A person who has infringed the provisions
of Articles 3 to 5 or of Article 7 shall be sentenced to penal
servitude not exceeding three months or a fine not exceed-
ing one hundred yen.
Any person attempting to commit the offenses defined
in the preceding paragraphs with the exception of Article 4,
shall be appropriately punished.
Article 16. A person who has instigated or assisted one
who is not a Japanese subject to manufacture or export or
import or sell or purchase or othervise give or receive
narcotics in violation of law shall be sentenced to penal
servitude not exceeding three months or a fine not exceed-
ing one hundred yen.
Article 17. The articles used in violating Articles 3
to 5 or of Article 7 may be detained or confiscated by adminis-
trative disposition, except when confiscated by judicial
procedure.
Article 18. A person who has infringed the provisions
of Articles 8 to 13, or has made false report, or has refused
the examination of books or narcotics by the authorities
concerned, shall be sentenced to a fine not exceeding one
hundred yen, or to detention.
Article 19. ▲ person who has acted in violation of
orders issued in accordance with the provisions of Article 14
shall be sentenced to detention or fine.
Article 20. In case the druggist is a minor or a person
adjudged incompetent the penalties mentioned in the two pre-
ceding Articles shall apply to his legal representative.
This does not, however, apply to a minor having equal part
as an adult in the management of the business.
Article 21.

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
Article 2l. In case his representative, head of his
family, or other members of his family, other person living
with his family, his employees, or other perwons engaged
infringe the provisions of this Ordinance or violate such
orders as may be issued according to this Ordinance, a
medical practitioner, or a dentist, or a veterinary surgeon,
or a pharmaceutist, or a druggist cannot evade the punish-
ment therefor because of such act having not been done
under his direction.
Article 22. In case the representative of a juridical
person or his employee or employees or other persons engaged
infringe the provisions of this Ordinance or such orders
as may be issued according to this Ordinance in connection
with the business carried on by that juridical person, the
penal clauses of this Ordinance shall be applied to the
representative of the said juridical person.
Additional Rule. This Ordinance shall take offect
from the 1st of July, 1936.

the applicants reside) in accordance with the pertinent
regulations or rules of Manchukuo which have been recognized
by the Imperial Japanese Government.
Article 7. A person desiring to import narcotics in
accordance with the provisions of the preceding Article
shall apply in writing to the Im erial Japanese consular
authorities of the place wherein he resides, mentioning
the following matters:
(1) Names of articles and quantity thereof;
(2) Object of importation;
(3) Name of consigner (in case of a juridical person
the name of that juridical person), and business
address;
(4) Period of importation;
(5) Route or manner of transportation;
(6) The place of import.
If, after receiving a permit, the person thus authorized
desires to alter one or other of the specifications mentioned
in (1) to (6) he shall obtain new permission as to the items
which he is desirous of changing.
When

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
When the authorities concerned have given permission
on the import of narcotics asmentioned in Paragraph 1 of
the preceding Article they shall issue to the applicant an
import permit and a certificate attesting to the permission
of import.
Article 8. A person who has imported narcotics with
the permission of the authorities concerned as mentioned
in the preceding Article shall report the fact to the Im-
perial Japanese consular authorities, producing at the same
time the copies of the import permit and the certificate
attesting to the permit of import affixed to the article
imported, or copy of any certificates attesting to change
of the destination.
In case the person who has obtained the permission
mentioned in the preceding Article has not imported the
article he shall return the import permit and the certifi-
cate attesting to the permission of import to the Imperial
Japanese consular authorities of the place in which he re-
sides, within ten days from the day when the period of im-
port expired.
A person who has carried or taken delivery of narcotics
from the places outside of the jurisdiction of the Imperial
Japanese consulate, or transported them to a place outside
the jurisdiction of the Imperial Japanese consulate, shall
report the fact within ten days to the Imperial Japanese
consuler authorities of the place wherein he resides.
Article 9. Pharmaceutists or druggists cannot sell
or otherwise hand over narcotics unless they receive from
medical practitioners, dentists, veterinary surgeons, phar-
maceutists, druggists, or hospitals, schools or officially
recognized foreign medical practitioners, dentists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists, or druggists bills of sale
certified by the Imperial Japanese consular anthorities.
This does not, however, apply when pharmaceutists sell or dis-
pense narcotics in accordance with prescriptions issued by
medical practitioners, dentists, or veterinary surgeons, or
officially recognized foreign medical practitioners, dentists
or veterinary surgeons.
In the bills of sale mentioned in the foregoing para-
graph there shall be included the name of the narcotics,
quantity thereof, object of use, date of purchase, place
of business of the purchaser, and purchaser's occupation and
name, and the seal of the purchaser shall be put to the
same.
In receiving or purchasing narcotics from officially
recognized foreign pharmaceutists or druggists the regula-
tions of Paragraph 1 shall equally apply.
The bills of sale or prescriptions shall be kept for
three

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
three years from the date of their issuance.
Article 10. In case medical practitioners, dentists,
veterinary surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists who dis-
continue their occupation or withdraw from Manchukuo
have in their possession unused narcotics, they shall
speedily transfer such narcotics to other medical practi-
tioners, dentists, veterinary surgeons, pharmaceutists or
druggists who are Japanese subjects.
In the aforementioned case, the tranaferer shall pre-
sent to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities a writ-
ten report mentioning therein the name of articles the
narcotics transferred, quantity thereof, place of business,
and date of transfer, such statement to be jointly signed
by the transferer and the transferee, with their seals being
put to the document.
In case such narcotics cannot be transferred according
to the provisions of Paragraph 1 of this Article the matter
shall be reported to the Imperial Japanese consular authori-
ties of the place where the transferer resides and be sub-
jeot to the direction of the authorities concerned.
Article ll. In case narcotics have been rendered use-
less on account of alteration or decomposition or on some
other account, medical practitioners, dentists, veterinary
surgeons, pharmaceutitsts or druggists shall report the matter
in writing to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities of
the place they reside in, mentioning therein the name of the
narcotics and quantity thereof, and producing at the same
time such narcotics.
In case a medical practitioner or a dentist, or a veteri-
nary surgeon, or a pharmaceutist or a druggist dies or be-
comes missing, whoever learns that the deceased or missing
medical practitioner or other person was in possession of a
stock of unused narcotics shall at once report the matter
to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities of the place
in which the deceased or missing person had resided.
As to the disposition of the narootics mentioned in the
two preceding paragraphs the matter shall be subject to the
direction of the Imperial Japanese consular authorities
concerned.
Article 12. Medicel practitioners, dentists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists shall distinguish
narcotics from other medicines or drugs and keep them in a
locked place.
Druggists cannot sell, distribute, or give narcotics in
small portions.
Artiole 13. Medical practitioners, dentists, veteri-
nary surgeons, pharmaceutists or druggists shall keep fixed
books

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
books in which they shall enter the amount or quantity,
eto., of consumption of narcotics, their sale and purchase,
transfer, etc., and keep such reocrds for three years from
the date of their entry. Besides, they shall make a table
showing the amount, etc., of narcotics puchased and sold
or received and transferred during each year and submit
the table to the Imperial Japanese consular authorities
concerned by the end of January of the following year.
The Imperial Japanese consular authorities concerned
may examine the books mentioned in the preceding paragraph,
or the narcotics kept by those mentioned in the preceding
paragraph.
Article 14. The Imperial Japanese consular authorities
may, in connection with the books mentioned in the preceding
Article and other matters, issue to the persons interested
such orders as deemed necessary for control.
Article 15. A person who has infringed the provisions
of Articles 3 to 5 or of Article 7 shall be sentenced to penal
servitude not exceeding three months or a fine not exceed-
ing one hundred yen.
Any person attempting to commit the offenses defined
in the preceding paragraphs with the exception of Article 4,
shall be appropriately punished.
Article 16. A person who has instigated or assisted one
who is nt a Japanese subject to manufacture or erport or
import or sell or purchase or otherwise give or receive
narcotics in violation of law shall be sentenced to penal
servitude not exceeding three months or a fine not exceed-
ing one hundred yen.
Article 17. The articles used in violating Articles 3
to 5 or of Article 7 may be detained or confiscated by adminis-
trative disposition, except when confiscated by judicial
procedure.
Artiole 18. A person who has infringed the provisions
of Articles 8 to 13, or has made false report, or has refused
the examination of books or narcotics by the authorities
concerned, shall be sentenced to a fine not exceeding one
hundred yen, or to detention.
Article 19. A person who has acted in violation of
orders issued in accordance with the provisions of Article 14
shall be sentenced to detention or fine.
Article 20. In case the druggist is a minor or a person
adjudged incompetent the penalties mentioned in the two pre-
ceding Articles shall apply to his legal representative.
This does not, however, apply to a minor having equal part
as an adult in the management of the business.
Article 21.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Artiole 2l. In case his representative, head of his
family, or other members of his family, other person living
with his family, his employees, or other persons engaged
infringe the provisions of this Urdinance or violate such
orders as may be issued according to this Ordinance, a
medical practitioner, or a dentist, or a veterinary surgeon,
or a pharmaceutist, or a druggist cannot evade the punish-
ment therefor because of such act having not been done
under his direction.
Article 2?. In case the representative of a juridical
person or his employee or employees or other persons engaged
infringe the provisions of this Ordinance or such orders
as may be issued according to this Ordinance in connection
with the business carried on by that juridical person, the
penal clauses of this Ordinance shall be applied to the
representative of the said juridical person.
Additional Rules. This Ordinance takes effect from
the 1st of July, 1936.
This Ordinance is enforced in all places except the South
Manchuria Railway Zone.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
THE JAPAN CHRONICLE - June 2, 1936.
Wz have on a previous occasion re-
marked on the possibility of other
"lifelines" besides Manchuria being dis-
covered, some of them dangling south-
wards, especially since Manchurla 1s
proving a little disappointing from an
economic point of view, and this is con-
fnrmed by the Mainichi. which says
In view of the very creditable show-
ing of our economic advance in the
South Seas, summary pessimism per-
haps may not be warranted, but con-
sidering the unfavourable reports that
come from almost all directions, we
would do well to prepare for the worst.
Surmounting trade difficulties from
without should be our primary concern
and the object of greatest effort to-day.
Recently the term "life-line" has
come to assume varied shades of mean-
ing Yet there is no truer "life-line"
for Japan than economic and commer-
clal activity. It is the basis of our
national existence; our main artery.
There can be no more urgent need
than to organise a large-scale commis-
sion to consider once and for all the
basic national policy in that connec-
tion. Needless to add. our economic
advance will be strictly peaceful.
In the event. however, that the powers
concerned are not disposed to respect
our life-line. and elect :o ignore our
right to existence. we must have at our
command sufficient means to make
them listen to reasen.
It is interesting to note the extremely
limited manner in which our contempor-
ary interprets the word "peaceful." So
long as Japan finds no opposition what-
ever to her economic programme, then its
realhsation will be peaceful: but if other
Powers put any obstacles in the way, then
Japan must compel them to listen to
reason. The last paragraph is a direct
contradiction of the one that went before
it. In an article on the drug problem in
the same issue of the paper, we find a
direct contradiction of statement with-
out even making another paragraph of it.
The traffic in narcotic drugs is a
serious problem not only in China.
Canada. and the United States. but in
Japan as well. Let it be known to the
world that the Japanese government,
with the full support of the national
sentiment, is doing its best to stop illicit
traffic in drugs. It might be added that
when it is to the interest of the nation
to curb the anti-social traffic, the Japan-
ese government will go ahead and do
it whether it is asked to act by the
League or not. To accuse Japan of
tolerating traffic in drugs is like ac-
cusing Japan of encouraging national
suicide.
In yet another place it says that no Gov-
ernment can control the traffic when the
smugglers are sufficiently determined. So
we have it all at once that Japan is
already doing her best; that when she
finds it to her interest to stop the traffic
she will do so; that it can't be done.
What it all means is past understanding.
But why should a charge of encouraging
the drug traffc in China be equivalent to
a charge of encouraging national suicide?
China's ills must eventually react un-
favourably on Japan, but in a too remote
manner to call it suicide for Japan. Per-
haps it is the language dificulty that leads
to this confusion of thought.

little practical difference in the present situation

will be noted, are quite inadequate to serve as
deterrents.

COPY
1 of 20 Marunouchi, 2 chome,
Kojimachi-ku, Tokyo,
September 12, 1936.
Sanitary Section
Metropolitan Police Office.
It is regrettable that cases of narcotic smuggling
involving Japanese vessels at rorts of the United States
have recently occurred in ranid succession. Prev ntive
measures previously precticed by the ships of this com-
pany are considered inadequate to the eradication of the
problem, and therefore it is deemed urgently necessary
to devise and enforce effective measures. The following
have therefore been put into force:
1. Aboard the CHICHIBU MARU, ASAIA LARU, TATSUTA IARU,
and TAIYC MARU on the service between liong Kong and San
Francisco, as well as orthe HIYE MARU, HIKAWA MARU, and
HEIAN MARU of the Kobe-Seattle service, either a first or
second mate in addition to the regular members of the crew
is attached to each ship in the capacity of a searching
officer, and a helmsman is attached to each as an assistant.
In addition to the searching and surveillance hitherto
practiced, the new searching officer and assistant are
engaged

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
encored exclusively in the effort to pr vont smuggling
of narcotics. Upon ar ival at Americ n ports their duty
is to keep close guard in co-operation with the regular
watchmon in the company's service in the United States.
2. While docked in American ports the landing of
nembers of the crew is under st ict supervision.
3. When any visitor wishes to see a member of the crew,
either a watchman or the helmsman reports the request to
the senior first mate for permission.
4. A correspondence box is provided in an appropriate
place on each ship.
5. Instructions have been issued to our company's
branch offices and a encies to the effect that the cards
hitherto granted to ship peddlers shall be henceforth changed
in form requiring that photographs of such ship peddlers
be attached.
6. The amount of reward offered to those who discover
narcotics on board is increased in order to encourage dis-
covery.
(signed) Section of Marine Transportation
Nippon Yusen Kabushiki Kaisha.

JAPAN CHRONICLE
December 2, 1936
DRUG SMUGGLERS
GANG OF FOURTEEN ARRESTED BY
OSAKA POLICE
Said to have smuggled out Y285,000
worth of narcotics in the past two years, a
group of 14 drug traffickers have been
arrested by theOsaka police
It is alleged that Selzaburo Osumi. an
Osaka resident. assisted by 13 Osaka and
Kobe residents. bought cocaine, morphine
and heroin and shipped these chemicals
to a certain Tokumatsu Hoshino. Takao.
Formosa. for illegal exports to South
China
Their turnover from February 1934 to
June last. when the police started activi-
ties. is put at Y285.000

THE JAPAN ADVERTISER
February 14, 1937.
POLICE HOLD 5O SUSPECTS AS SELLERS OF NARCOTICS
Arrest of "Contractor" Leads to Detention
Of Large Group in Osaka and Kobe
More than 50 suspects have been arrested in Osaka
and Kobe in conneotion with the sale of more than
¥200,000 worth of narcotios in Kyushu, the Kwansai
and the Kwanto, according to Domei. The sales had
been made during the past few years.
Police uncovered the activities of the group
after Mr. Hanazono Bando, 47, supposedly a building
contraotor at Osaka, was detained by the Wakamatsu
city water police and the Fukuoka criminal affairs
section operatives. Mr. Bando's confession led to
the arrest of Mr. Tadashi Naito, 4l; Mr. Yoshitaro
Isono, 52, and others. Mr. Bando is alleged to have
been in charge of operations in Myushu; Mr. Naito, in
the Kwansai, and Mr. Isono, in the Kwanto. Supolies
of cooaine, heroin and other narcotios were obtained
at two Osaka pharmacies.

GENEVA, Apr11 20th, 1937.
On June 25th 1936, 16 Chinese stokers of the Dutch
ship "Serooskerke" anchored in Yokchama harbour were arrested
by the Marine Police in the act of smoking and eating opium
on board their ship. The ship had left Hong Kong on June 8th
and had called at Shangha1, Dairen, Weihaiwe1 and Moji.
Some utensils, a spirit lamp, 5 grammes of prepared
opium and 20 grammes of dross, were seized. The men declared
that they had bought the utensils and the spirit lamp in
Shungha1 and 500 grammes of prepared opium in Hong Kong. The
origin of the prepared opium 1s unknown. 14 of the men were
released, 2 were transferred to the Office of the Public
Prosecutor of the Yokohama Court of First Instance, and, on
June 30th, the Court d ismissed the charge.
(Signed) M. YOKOYAMA

Secretary of State,

159, June 5, 2 p. m.
One. As a result of reports at Geneva on the
narcotic situation in North China, the new chief of the
Japenese consular police at Peiping stated yesterday
1086
in reply to questions of foreign press correspondents
that about 200 Japanese subjects in Peiping had been
prosecuted during the past year on charges arising out of
the manufacture, sale and transportation of narcotics,
that about l00 had been deported, and that others had
been fined. The correspondents differed as to whether he
894.114 an.
said that any had been imprisoned. He also stated that
many Chinese attempted to pass as Koreans and, after
considerable questioning, that he believed that more
Chinese than Japanese were engaged in the drug traffic.
According to the correspondents, an official of the
Japanese Embassy stated that everything possible was
being done to suppress the activities of Japanese and
Korean drug manufacturers and traders but that it was
difficult because some alternative occupation had to be
found for those engaged in the trade as otherwise the
authorities

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.97)
FS
2-No. 159, June 5, 2 p. m. from Peiping
authorities would find themselves w ith a number of idle
undesirables on their hands. The official was further
reported as saying that deportation was not always a
solution as it frequently meant merely the transfer of the
problem from one place to another and that the Japanese
authorities aimed at the gradual absorption of these
persons into other modes of life.
Two. There is reason to believe that the Japanese
interested in the question of the suppression of dealers
ueeronality
of Japanese rationals in narcotics in Peiping are in dis-
agreement on the subject and that this disagreement makes
it impossible for those Japanese who would like to see
effective suppression without delay.

O
Mr. John W. Bulkley, Customs Agency Jervice, Treasury
Department, came in this morning andshowed me a letter
just received from the Treasury representative at Kobe,
Japan. In this letter it was stated that in 1933 the
Consul at Kobe informed the Treasury representative that
he would assist in obtaining information from the
Japanese police in regar to the movementsof narcutic
()
traffickers and contraband of interest to the United
States. in April, 1937, however, the Treasury renre-
(C)
sentative had received letters from the Consuls at Kobe
Ci
and Yokohama quoting from an insiruction from the Embassy
11
at Tokyo to the effect that henceforth all information
requested by the Treasury representative at Kobe should
be obtained from the Japanese authorities through the
American Embassy at Tokyo.
Mr. Bulkley called attention to the recent agreement
between the Treasury and State Departments and expressed
the opinion that the instructions from the Embassy at
Tokyo were a violation of the agreement. I replied that
the Treasury must bear in mind that in Japan there existed
JUN 11 1937
FILKD

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
a special arrangement with regard to the obtainin: of
information in connection with drug-smu ling operations
and that with the best will in the worl: there were cer-
tain things which the American diplomatic and consular
representatives could not do. I said that my understand-
ing was that the Japanese authorities insisted that all
requests for information on this subject be transmitted
by the American Embassy to the Japanese Foreign Office
or, in some cases, to the Home Office.
After Mr. Bulkley nad returned to his office he
telephoned to say that he wished me to take no action
on his remarks, that he was goin, to leave the matter
to Mr. Graves to handle, and that possibly Mr. Graves
might decide to comimunicate with Dr. Hornbeck to see
what, if anything, could be done to secure greater
cooperation in Japan between the representatives of our
respeotive departments.
A memorandum summarizing correspondence in regard
to the manner in which the cooperation of the J panese
authorities may be sought by representatives of the State
and Treasury Departments in Japan is attached hereto.
The record shows that on several occasions the State
Department, through its representatives in Tokyo, has
made every effort to secure the approval of the Japanese
authorities

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
authorities to a plan whereby closer direct cooperation
might be effected between the appropriate officials of
Japan and the United States having charge of the control
of the illicit traffic in narcotic drugs. When the subject
was last taken up with the Japanese Foreign Office, the
Chief of the Treaty Bureau stated on Noveber 30, 1936,
that should the Treasury representative desire information
in regard to drug-smuggling operations, he must ask it of
the Foreign Office and not of the Department of Home Affairs,
local police officials, or private individuals. In 1933,
the Embassy at Tokyo was directed to instruct all Consular
officers in Japan to refer to the Embassy requests of
Colleotors of Customs for investi ation of narcotics
seizures and that arrangement is still in effect. Thus,
it will be seen that the activities of a Treasury represen-
tative in Japan are limited by restrictions imposed by
the Japanese officials. Those restrictions also apply to
representatives of the Department of State and must be
taken into consideration in connection with the fulfilment
of the agreement with the Treasury Department, as outlined
in Diplomatic Serial No. 2784, dated May 26, 1937, copy of
which is attached hereto.

Subject: Narco ic Investi. ations in Japan.
Sir:
Reference is respectfully maue to previous corre-
spondence re the above, particularly to report of Jan-
uary 16, 1934, signed by the writer anc Treasur, Attac e
Martin R. Nicholson.
Reference i: furtner made to letter of May 18, 1933,
from the Under Secretary of the reasury L. W. Robert, Jr.,
to the Secretary of State, wherein the Treasury Departmernt
agreed that all requests for investi ations of narcotic
drugs whicn required the assistance of the J panese
authorities should e presented to the Japanese Forein
Office by the American Embassy at Tokyo.  In part, this
letter read as follows:
"in view of your statement that conditions existin,
at the present time make it advisable that requests
to the Japanese Government for narcotic investi ations
should be presented to the Japanese Foreign Office
by the American Embassy at Tokyo, this Department
is willing to acquiesce".
The question arose as to whether the above was broad
enough to cover all routine requests for information us to
arrivals, movements and departures of narcotic traffickers,
as distinct from investigations into specific sruggling
operations. After consultation with the Embassy, it was
understood that such requests for observation of the move-
ments of such traffic suspects could continue to be addressed
to the local police authorities throuh the various
Consulates. Accordingly, from 1933 to date this has been
done.
Such

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Such requests have been particularly directed to the
observing and reporting on movenents of individuals at
the instance of the Trecsury At ache in snan hai, an: we
have, as a rule, been able to cooperate wi n ir. Nicholson
to the extent of advising lim of arrivals, departures, ete.,
of persons in whom he was interested.
Now we are in receipt of leter from tne American
Consul at Kobe to the effect that this arrangement can no
longer be effective. A similar letter was received from
the American Consul at Yokohama. Copy of letter from the
Kobe Consul is attached l.eret).
We would take this oucasion to point ous that ex-
perience has shown that, as a eneral proposition, it will
be hopeless to rely on requests throuh the Embassy for the
purpose of keeping track on the movements of narcotic
traffickers in Japan. This for the reason that by the time
& request is forwarded the Embassy and then addres. ed to
the Foreign Office, and then sent y tom thr u. chan els
and a reply received, tne infornation i: received too late
to be of any use. As ai. example of this in Au. ust, 1935,
we wrote the Emoasey requestin to oe advised as to the
movements of one Anto.. Wirthau ler. Reply to this was
received on Cctober a, 19e, w.ile Wirthmull'r h:d actually
left Japan on Au ust 8th.
In view of the new sit lation, there are uwo courses
that we can oursue in tis tte.
(1) Continue to secure wnat information we can
on such narcotic traffickers from steomship compa ies,
hotels, etc., or
(2) Aoproach the local police ourselves, without
the mediation of tne local Consulates.
Under the first course, with our limited personnel
and no money to pay for infomation, result: will probably
not be what they should.
In view of previous correspondence witn the State
Department, we do not feel we can aiopt the second course
without authority from our Department. We do not know
whether the State Department will object to our aoing so
or not, as without an expression from the Department, we
will not broach the subject at this end.
The

-2-

Letter from Mr. Graves dated May 28.
