Since Mr. Graves' letter is marked "Personal and
Confidential", I would su est t.at you invite him to
come to your office for a discussion of the subject.
After that discussion, it can be deterained whether a
written reply to Mr. (raves' letter should be made.
After the conclusion of the areement with the
1
Treasury Department on March 25, 1937, no instruction
7
O
on the subject was sent to the Embassy at Tokyo until
Diplomatic Serial No. 2784, dated April 28, 1937, which
embodied the agreement, was prepared. That Serial was
sent to the Embassy at Tokyo and to the Consulates in
Japan in the pouch which 1eft here May 17, 1937. The
pouch is due in Tokyo next week, about June 8. It
would appear, therefore, that the Embassy's instruction,
dated April 19, 1937, was sent to the Consuls in Japan
without special instructions from the Department. These
JUN 11 1937
instructions
FILED

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
instruotions were probably issued in an effort to
clarify the Department's instructions of March 27.
1933 (See page 4 of the attached summary), which directed
the Embassy to "instruct all Consular officers in Japan
to refer to the Embassy requests of Collectors of Customs
for investigation of narcotics seizures." According to
the letter of Treasury representative Martin G. Scott,
dated April 26, 1937, the question arose as to whether
these instructions covered routine requests for informa-
tion as to arrivals, movements and departures of narcotic
traffickers. After consultation with the Embassy, it was
understood that such requests for observation of the
movements of such traffic suspects could continue to be
addressed to the local police authorities throuh the
various Consulates. It is possible that the Embassy's
instructions of April 19, 1937, were based upon the oral
reply of Mr. Kuriyama of the Japanese Foreign Office to
Mr. Dickover, American Chargé, on November 30, 1936, to
the effect that a narcotics representative of the Treasury
may reside in Japan but must deal primarily with the
appropriate officials of the Foreign Office and if he
through the aaerucan Entesy
desires information he must ask it of the Foreign Office
and not of the Department of Home Affairs, local police
officials or private inaividuals.
In

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
In transmitting that information to the Treasury
Department on December 3, 1936, the Department tated
that, in the light of the views expressed by the Forein
Office and of other factors in connection with our
relations with Japan, it appears to us that it would
not be expedient for this Government formally to request
of the Japanese Government permission to station in Japan,
for the handling of matters which involve narcotic drugs,
a new and special representative as suggested ; and it seems
to us advisable to continue to handle through the Embassy,
as at present, the exchange of information in regard to the
illicit traffic in narcotic drugs.
I would suggest that a telegram be prepared informing
the Embassy at Tokyo of Mr. Graves' letter and requesting
the Embassy's comments in regard thereto.
G. A. Morlock.

May 11, 1937.
The Commissioner f Customs,

Treasury Departnent,

Subject: Narcotic Investigations in Japan.
Sir:
Reference is respectfully nade to our letter of April 26, 1937,
re the above.
On May 8th this office was visited by Police Captain T. Yamaguchi,
Mr. I. Hiura and Mr. Z. Kuninasa, all of the detective bureau of the
Hyogo prefectural police. Captain Yama uchi is in ch-rge of the nar-
cotic control section of his Bureau. Th- visit was in connection with
the Tensaka case in Seattle, the above fficers having been assigned
to the case in connection with the movenents of Toshiteru Nonaka, be-
lieved to be an acconplice f Tensaka. This visit was unsolicited by
this office.
After discussing various aspects f the case, Captain Yamaguchi
advised that Nonska had not as yet been apprehended, iving as the
reason therefor the there ned been o much delay in notifying his
office that this arty had hed tine to mak? :ood his get-away. We
advised Captain Yamaguchi thst we hsd duly reported the matter to our
Embassy for transnission to the roper Japanese authorities through
the Foreign Office. Captain Yamsguchi replied that that method, be-
cause of Governnent red tape, lways resulted in considerable delay
in tnat the matter : asses from our Embass. to the Foreign Office, from
thence to the Home Office; and from thence to the prefectural police;
he requested that in future we get in touch with his office at once,
whereupon they would be in a position to act promptly and apprehend
such individuals before they had an opportun'ty to escape. He further
stated that if we would advise him on such future ccasions either by
phone or a personal call, prior to reporting to the Embassy, he be-
lieved that beneficial results could be obtained. We thanked these
gentlemen for their visit and their interest in the case, and advised
them we would refer the matter to our Government. Assuring us of their
desire and willingness to cooperate full: with us in apprehending in-
dividuals connected with the illicit narcotic trade, these gentlemen
then took their departure.

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The above discussion was  uite informal, and we do not kmow if these
gentlemen would desire their views to be made a matter of diplomatic cor-
respondence. The foregoing is tnerefore submitted primarily for the De-
partment's consideration when replying to our letter of April 26th.
It is the writer's understanding that one of the reasons assigned in
1933 for directing all requests for investigations through our Embassy was
that the local authorities would not ive us any information. Whatever
the situation was at that time, it appears to have changed. Whether this
is because the Japanese steamship conpanies, apprehensive of fines, have
taken a new interest in the matter, or for other reasons, need not be
discussed here.
We wish to make our position quite clear, - we have no desire what-
ever to embarrass our State Departaent in any way, or to unduly influence
the Bureau in this matter. We are the Departnent's agents in the field
and will carry out any instructions issued to the best of our ability.
However, to put the matter frankly, unless there is sone diplomatic or
other reason of which we are not aware, we believe the plan sug ested by
Captain Yamaguchi should be given a trial.
If we are authorized to cooper:te with local Japanese cfficials, we
would suggest that a small sum of money be given us, which we vould not
have to account for in the isual accointing rocedure, which can be used
should the occasion arise. This umount need not be large, $looo would be
sufficient as a beginning, that we may determine whether the same may be
spent to advantage or not. It ould not be our purpose to put any informers
on the payroll, but we shouli have a fund to pay for information if the
local uthorities consider the same advisable, or to meet other eaergencies.
If this is not considered advisable, we would suggest that Treasury Attache
Nicholson be authorized to advance this office, from time to time, such
sums as may be required. Copy of this report is being forwarded Treasury
Attache Nicholson, who is familiar with the narcotic situation in Japan,
for such comaents as he may care to make to this office and the Bureau in
connection therewith.
Respectfully,
(Signed)
MARTIN G. SCOTT
Treasury Attache

Receipt is res ectfully acknowledged of copy of criminal case report
on Tensaka, nailed is fron Seattle under d.te of April 29th. For the
infornation of th Supervising Custoas Agent I have the honor to advise
the following, as furnished this ffice by the local police authorities:
A nation wide hunt for T shiteru Nonaka ha: been under way since
early May. Nonakt was finally parehended in Dairen on May 28th, and
arrested at his hiding place la4 Chigusa-cho, Dairen. A police officer
was sent from Kobe on June lst to bring Nonuka back to Kobe. He will
be due here about June loth. Nonaka's brother las also been arrested
and is now undergoing exanination. The police advise they have been
successful in tracing the n venents of both Non ka and Tensaka, in and
around Tokyo, prior to the shipnent of the narcotics in question.
The police investigations indicite thet Nonaka is a member of a
criminal outfit, itn membership in Dairen, Tientsin, Shimonoseki,
Fukuoka, Ehime, Yamanashi, etc. A number of individuals believed to
be menbers of Nonaka's gang ve lready been taken into custody, and
a furtner round-up is anticipated when the guestioning of Nonaka has
been completed. The police advise they will furnish us detailed inforna-
tion when their investigation is completed. It is presuned this informe-
tion will be made available to Seattle through the usual diplomatic
channels, but this is written as being possibly a more prompt transmittal
of the same.
Incidentally for the infornation of the Department, the Kobe Customs
have recently established a special narcotic force to cope with narcotic
smuggling and trans-shipment at this port. Six officers have been assigned
to this new unit.

FILED
(a) Those of a trivial noture involving check un
of suspected individuals at hotels, steamers, local

(b) Investigations of Japanese subjects, individually
unimportant, or others suspected of operating on a small

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
MJD --2-- No. 178, July 6, 1 p. m. from Tokyo
information is promptly required to be of value.
(c) Questions of a delicate nature such as those

illegal diversion of legally manufactured goods, cases
which develop a delioate angle with the Japanese or some
feature of a delicate nature from our angle.
Three. The arrangement formulated in Kobe contem-
plates that inquiries in group (a) are to be handled
directly by the office of the Treasury representative,
with its established unofficial contacts in Japan. In-
quiries falling under group (b) to be handled by t!
Consulate. Tne Consulate to use its own discretion as to
manner of tal ing up such cases with Japanese authorities.
Queries falling under group (c) to be referred to the
Embassy without any action locally. All narcotic question:
to be first discussed cooperatively and sympathetically
between Consul and Treasury representative, and after such
discussion appropriate way of handling to be determined
in accordance with above plan. It is understood that the
method suggested is only a guide and that each case is to
be considered on its merits. It is also understood that
proper caution will be exercised in differentiating between
categories (b) and (c) and any questions of a delicate
natare would be referred to the Embassy before any action
is taken locally.
Four. The Embassy is of the opinion that only those
cases

In regard to paragraph 5 of Tokyo's telegram,
No. 178 of July 6, it is true that the first paragraph
of page 2 of Diplomatic Serial No. 2784 of April 28,
1937 does not specifically except Japan. That paragraph
reads:
"The Bureau of Narcotics remains the Special
Administration of the United States under the
Narcotics Limitation Convention of 193l and will
continue to be the agency in the United States
responsible for the direct exchange with corres-
ponding foreign administrations of information
and evidence with reference to the illicit traffic
in narcotic drugs. This will not, however, be
considered as excluding the exchange of informa-
tion and evidence directly between Treasury
representatives abroad and the police and other
overnmental authorities of foreign countries, as
outlined above."
My comments follow:
Paragraph one.
The Department's instruction of March 27, 1933 to
the Embassy reads:
"Instruct all consular officers in Japan to
refer to the Embassy requests of Collectors of
Customs for investigation of narcotics seizures."
According to Treasury representative Scott's letter
of April 26, 1937 to the Treasury Department, the question
arose as to whether the above instruction was broad
encough to cover all routine requests for information
as to arrivals, movements and departures of narcotic
traffickers,

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
traffickers, as distinct from investi ations into specific
smuggling operations. After consultation with the Embassy,
he says, it was understood that such requests for ob-
servation of the movements of such traffic suspects could
continue to be addressed to the local police authorities
through the various Consulates. Accordingly, from l933
to date this has been done, he added. It appears that
the Embassy's iastruction of Anril 19, 1937 to the Con-
sular fficers in Japan was issued in oraer to rescind
the instruction of 1933 referred to in this paragraph.
My interpretation of the Department's instruction
of April 10, 1935 is that all inquiries of the 1ocal
fin offi..alacthon
police come under this order. I have consulted Mr.
Anslinger, Commissioner of the Bureau of Narcotics, and
he says that under the Direct Exchange Agreement with
Japan of 1928 he does not exchange any information
directly with the Japanese administrative officials.
His understanding is that everything should go through
the American Embassy at Tokyo.
I should like to point out, however, that under
the Department's instruction of April 10, 1935, the
consular officers may address the local authorities
in certain cases if so instructed by the Embassy.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

Tan
MEMORANDUM
August 3, 1937.
The agreement to which Ambassador Grew refers
in numbered paragraph five of his telegram No. 235
of July 30, 10 a.m. 1s the executive agreement entered
into with the Japanese Government July 6, 1928, at
the request of the Treasury Department.
This agreement provided that the exchange of in-
formation and evidence with reference to persons
engaged in the illicit traffic should take place through
the Japanese Foreign Office.
In a supplementary agreement of September 6, 1929
(which dealt with cooperation between consular officers
and the authorities of the countries in which they are
stationed), the Japanese Government consented to permit
American consular officers in Japan proper to communicate
infomation of the kind under discussion directly to_
the Foreign Office, or, "in case of special urgency"
to the local Governor or Superintendent of Customs.
It did not authorize consular officers to ask local
officials for information.
The

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The situation became such that, in March 1933,
the Embassy suggested, as a method of procedure more
likely to ensure action, that all requests to the
authorities in Japan for investigations or information
in narcotics cases should be presented by tre Embassy
to the prescribed official in the Foreign Office. To
this the Treasury agreed.
FE:SJF:AT

American Consulate
Kobe, Japan,
July 14, 1937.
The Honorable
Joseph C. Grew,
American Ambassador,
Tokyo.
Sir:
I have the honor to acknowledge your letter of July 18
1937, with reference to the suggested plan agreed upon by
the Treasury representative and this Consulate, for handling
requests for information on narcotic matters. It is noted
that you enclosed for comment a copy of the Department's
telegram No. 98 of June 30, 1 p.m., in which reference is
made to a report of the Treasury representative in Kobe to
the effect that a snecial detail of six officers has been
established to cope with illicit drug traffic in Kobe, and
that local police authorities have furnished the Treasury
representative with i: formation regarding suspected traf-
fickers.
The Department's telegram was appar ntly called forth
by the last paragraph of a routine rpport of June 3, 1937,
from the Treasury represent tive. The paragraph in question
reads as follows:
1"
Incidentally for the information of the
Department, the Kobe Customs have recently
established a special narcotic force to cope
with narcotic smuggling and transshipment at
this port. Six officers have been assigned to this
new unit."
An investigation of this matter indicates that on
May 17th a slight reorganization in the surveillance div-
ision of the customs service took place. As a result
of this reorganization, six officers of the Criminal Section
were assigned to the investigation of narcotic cases. This
assignment, however, involved no increase in personnel, and,
while perhaps conducive to greater efficiency, does not
alter materially the handling of narcotic cases on the part
of local officials. As of possible interest, there is
enclosed a memorandum of the investigation made by Vice
Consul Schuler.
With

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
With regard to the reference made in the Department's
telegram to the receipt of informetin directly from the
customs uuthorities by the Trea ury representative, it is
my opinion that while this may have taken p lace and been
technically contrary to the previous arrangement for co-
ordination of information in Tokyo, the point is not of
major importance since, as soon as a local coordination plan
is worked out, further difficulties of this sort should be
obviated.
The above mentioned reorganization of the custons serv-
ice was not reported to the Embassy since I had no knowledge
of the matter until the receipt of the Embassy's letter
of July 8th. It should be understood t hat the Treasury rep-
resentative considered it of so little importance that he did
not happen to mention it in cur various conversations on nar-
cotic matters. As has been noted, he referred to it as an
incidental comment in a routine report. In my opinion, for
some reason or cth r, the Department has been somewhat misled
by the report in question, and has apparently attached much
more importance to the statements made by the Treasury repre-
sentative then he intended or was warranted. It should be
made clear that I have comilete confidence in the willing-
ness of the Treasury represent: tive to cooperate in every way

=== Page 283 === (confidence 0.99)
July 14, 1937.
MEMORANDUM
According to informetion furnished by the Imperial
Customs at Kobe, the special investigation of narcotic
cases prior to May 17 of this year has been entrusted to
the Criminal Secticn of the Surveillance Division of the
Customs. This section, composed of sixteen members, had
been handling prior to the date given above all custom
cases in which criminal intent appeared. Thus, any of
the sixteen members of the section might be charged with
the duty of investigating a case of narcotic smugeling.
Routine examinaticns were, and still are, undertaken by
a detail of one hundred and fifty guards composing the
Police Affairs Section also under the Surveillance Division.
On May 17, 1937, allegedly to improve efficiency within the
Criminal Section six officers of that group were assigned
to the investigation of narcotic cases alone. No additions
in personnel have been made, however. It should also be
emphasized that these six officers act upon the advice
of the Police Affairs Section; they do not initiate in-
vestigations themselves. The official who furnished
this information would not comment on the effect the
reorganization might have on the control of the drug
traffic in Kobe harbor.

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
Statement.
At the Yokohama Customs a number of specially
commissioned officers have been, and continue to be,
engaged in the suppression of the narcotics traffic
and similar activities. The following practice is pur-
sued.
(1) Even such narcotics, drugs, etc. as are
legitimately loaded on board vessels mooring in Yokohama
harbor are subjected to rigid supervision. Especially
is this the case with vessels navigating from the China
coast to North America.
(2) Supervision is rigidly exercised over passengers,
particularly Chinese passengers and those who call on
Chinese passengers.
(3) A list is kept of persons suspected of being
narcotics smugglers, or previously arrested or prosecuted
therefor, and the special officers keep a sharp lookout
for those persons, in cooperation with the other Customs
officers.
(4) Responsible members of the crews of ships are
induced to make search for smuggled narcotics, etc. The
special officers, in close touch with the Yokohama Water
Police, exercise rigid supervision on board vessels.
By these methods, discovery and arrest of smugglers
is actively prosecuted.
At the Kobe Customs, six specially commissioned
officers have been appointed and attached to the bureau
of

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of surveillance and other sections in order to further
strengthen the rigid control of narcotics smugling.
They are exclusively engaged in control of sauggling of
narcotics. A smuggling suppression section has also been
inaugurated in the same bureau. The following methods
have been adopted:
(1) The special officers, while endeavoring to
detect the movements of suspects, habitually board vessels
entering and sailing from the port of Kobe, and k ep a
sharp lookout over goods loaded which are regarded as
having some connection with such suspects, over cargo
unloaded, and over members of the crew and passengers.
(2) If necessary, the special officers participate
in inspection of the entire vessel, or in ins ection of
personal effects of passengers.
(3) Goods found through investigation and inquiries
made by the various sections of the hobe Customs are
submitted to the new suggling suppression section, while
reports received by the latter section are distributed
among other sections concerned.
(4) Cooperation between the police authorities, the
public procurator's office, and other Government offices
concerned is close.
(5) Investigation is occasionally made as to status
of transactions in narcotics, etc., on markets, and also
of other matters which vill serve as reference, and at the
end

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
end of every month a eport on conditions and results
of official ctivities pertaining to the suppression
of smuggling is presented to the director of the bureau
of surveillance.
(6) Laws and ordinances and trecties relating
to the control and suppression of narcotics, etc. are
closely studied. Besides, a li t of s ug ling suspects
is made up and materials necessary for the control of
narcotics, etc. are collected.
By these methods, a determined effort is being
made to trace and arrest smugglers of narcotics,etc.
At the Osaka Customs, specially conn.issioned of"icers
are appointed and attaci d to the bureau of surveillance.
In concert with the police authorities they are exclu-
sively engaged in detecting the s ug :ling of narcotics,
etc., and in collecting pertinent reports. For the
purpose of making the control and supervision of narcotics,
etc., more effective than ever before, all branches of
the Osaka Customs are instructed to exercise rigid
supervision of narcotics, etc.
At the Nagasaki Customs, watch stations have been
established on the docks and are enforcing rigid in-
spection of exports and personal effects of passengers
and thereby exercising control of smuggling of narcotics,
etc. Further, all other sections concerned are instructed
to

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
to exercise control over the smuggling of narcotics, etc.,
at all times.
At the Moji Customs, control and supervision are
particularly directed toward the ports of akamatsu
and Tokuyama for the reason that steamers arriving in
Moji generally stay for only a few hours. In control
of the traffic the followin methods are enforced at the
Moji Customs and its subsidiary ofrices:
(1) Chief officers in charge at the various branches
and agencies meet at the oji Cuotoms every other month
and discuss measures to be followed.
(2) For vessels proceeding to China, India, and
America, a rigid inspection is enforced on board, while
articles owned by, or entrusted to, members of the crew
of vessels are subjected to rigid control. Captains
and other orficers of vessels are also requested to
cooperate.
(3) A list of the names of dealers who habitually
visit vessels and sell various articles is made up and
"communication cards" are handed to dealers when the latter
wish to visit foreign vessels, thereby subjecting illegal
transactions to control.
(4) A list of the names of merchandise and dealers
in drugs in Moji and other ports under the jurisdiction
of the Moji Customs is made up and their movements are
always being watched. Supervision is also exercised over
goodg

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
goods of certain descriptions. By these methods, efforts
are made to disclose the smug ling of narcotics.
At the Hakodate Customs, specially commissioned officers
are appointed for the supervision of narcotics, etc., be-
sides control and supervision over the smuling of general
commodities. The following methods have been adopted:
(1) On vessels proceeding to North America, vessels
on which narcotics have previously been suggled, and
vessels on which narcotics suugling offenders and the
like are serving as members of the. crew, rigid inspection
is made of rooms and of articles belonging to members of
the crew as we l as of articles for the ships' use.
(2) Rigid inspection is made on board vessels coming
from China and Vladivostok.
(3)  sharp lookout is kept over the movements
of dealers who visit such vessels for the vending of
various articles, and over men on cargo-boats and other
visitors.
(4) A vigilant watch is always kept over the movements
of members of the crew of such vessels who come ashore.
(5) Close cooperation between the Customs and the
police authorities is maintained. By these methods, efforts
are made to disclose illegal transactions and arrest of-
fenders.

TRES TENDANT A EMPECHER L'UTILISATION DES NAVIEES
LANSOCEANIQUES POUR LE TRAFIC ILLICITE ET SURVEILLANCE
DES GRANDS PORTS DE MER.
I
Mosurls prises par la compagnie de navigation "Nippon Yusen
Kaisha" pendant 1936.
1. Inspeotions et mesures de surveillance à bord des navires.
a) Des inspections journalières sont exécutées pendant
la traversée et une inspection générale st afectuée avant
l'arrivée aux ports de Honolulu et de San Francisco.
b) A bord des navires cités ci-après s'embarquent un
officier de perquisition (searching officer), officier de
ler ou 2ème rang, avec un assistant (un pilote) chargés uni-
quement de dépister le trafic illicite des stupéfiants.
Cet officier exécute des perquisitions en collaboration avee
les officiers de ces navires.
Sur la ligne Hong-Kong - San Francisco:
Chichibu-Maru Asama-maru
Tatsuta-maru Taiyo-maru
Sur la ligne Kobe - Seattle:
Hiei-maru
Hikawa-maru
Heian-maru
o) Boftes à renseignements.
Sur tous les navires doit etre installée, à un endroit
approprié, une bofte à lettre destinée à recevoir les ren-
seignements que des hommes d'équipage auraient pu recueillir
et qui pourraient aider à la saisie de stupéfiants.
d) Récompenses.
Le montant des récompenses allouées aux informateurs
à bord a été augmenté en 1936.
2. Mesures prises à bord des bateaux faisant escale dans
les ports.

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
a) un officier et un pilote assurent la surveillance à
des bateaux à toute heure du jour et pendant la nuit un
ricier et deux pilotes.
b) pendant que les bateaux font escale a Hongkong et à
Shanghai 3 gardiens indiens sont adjoints à l'officier de
surveillance.
o) dans les ports du Japon la compagnie N.Y.K. demande
aux autorités d'envoyer 2 agents de police à bord.
d) pendant la nuit une seule porte d'entrée est ouverte.
Deux lampes d'une puissance de 2700 W sont allumées à babord
et à tribord du navire, en outre toutes les lampes pour les
nots de sauvetage sont allumées.
e) visiteurs.
Les hommes de l'équipage ne sont pas autorisés à recevoir
de visiteur dans l'admission de l'officier de lère classe.
f) certificats de marchands à bord.
Une formule plus stricte que par le passé a été adoptée
pour les certificats que la compagnie maritime delivre aux
commergants qui désirent monter à bord: a'après cette nouvelle
formule chaque certificat doit indiquer le nom du porteur
et etre accompagné a'une photographie.
g) dans les ports américains.
Pendant que les navires font escale dans les ports
du continent de l'Amérique du Nora, une surveillance très
striote est observée en ce qui concerne le débarquement
des équipages.

Aotivités du "Conaell maritime" (Kalsi-Kiogikai) à Kobé.
Le "Conseil maritime" composé de 5 représentants des
armateurs

armatours et 14 représentants des membres d'équipage a
tudié la question du controle du trafic illicite des

a) chaque armateur sera tenu de fmire des rapports
concernant les équipages trafiquants.
b) une surveillance plus stricte que par le passé
sera exercée dans le port.
c) des inspections rigoureuses seront exécutées à

pour le controle du trafic illicite des drogues
nuisibles.
f) la question du trafic illicite des stupéfiants du
Japon aux Etats-Unis sera étudiée avec une attention
spéciale.
III
Organimation et mesures prises par les autorités notament
dans les préfectures de Kanagawa, Osaka. Hyogo et Fukuoka.
(Les ports de Yokohama, Osaka, Kobe et .oji, Wakamatsu,

douanes,

Les quartiers chinois des grands ports sont surveillés

c) Lidte noire et photographies.

Une étroite collaboration est organisée entre les

cernant la surveillance rigoureuse des bagages des passa-
gers et des hommes d'équipage.

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.97)
-5 -
•) Inspeotion avant le départ.

ou de la Chine.

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
MEASURES DESIGNED TO PREVENT THE USE OF TRANSOCEANIC
STEAMERS
FOR
THE ILLICIT
TRAFFIC
AND
FOR
SURVEILLANCE
OF PHINCIPAL SEAPORTS
I.
Measures taken by the steamship company "Nippon Yusen
Kaisha" during 1936.
1. Inspections and measures of surveillance on board
ships.
(a) Daily inspections are carried out during a
voyage and a general inspection is effected before arrival
in the ports of Honolulu and San Francisco.
(b) On board the ships mentioned below, there is
embarked a searching officer, an officer of first or
second grade, with an assistant (a quartermaster),
whose sole duty is to detect illicit traffic in nar-
cotic drugs. This officer carries out searches in
collaboration with the officers of these ships:
On the Hong Kong-San Francisco line:
Chichibu Maru
Asama Maru
Tatsuta Maru
Taiyo Maru
On the Kobe-Seattle line:
Hiei Maru
Hikawa Maru
Heian Maru
(c) Report boxes.
On all the ships there is required to be installed
in an appropriate place a letter box designed to receive
information which men of the crew are able to collect
and which could aid in the seizure of narcotic drugs.
(d) Rewards.
The

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
The total of rewards allotted to informers
on board was increased in 1936.
2. Measures taken on board boats lying in port.
(a) An officer and a quartermaster ensure
surveillance on board of the boats at all hours
of the day and during the night an officer and
two quartermasters.
(b) While the boats are lying in port at
Hong Kong anu at Shanghai, three Indian guards
are added to the staff at the disposition of the
officer in charge of surveillance.
(c) In Japan ports the Hippon Yusen Kaisha
Company requests the authorities to send two police
officers on board.
(d) Durin the night only one gangway is open.
Two lamps of 2700 watt power are kept 1ighted on
the port and starboard sides of the ship. Further-
more, all the lifeboat lamps are lighted.
(e) Visitors.
The men of the crew are not permitted to receive
visitors without the permission of an officer of the
first class.
(f) Certificates of peddlers on board.
A procedure stricter than has been followed in
the past has been adopted for the certificates which
the shipping company gives to merchants (peddlers)
who desire to go on board. According to this new
procedure, each certificate must show the name of
the bearer and must be acoompanied by a photograph.
(g)

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
(g) In American ports.
While the ships are lying in the ports of the
North American continent, a very strict surveillance
is observed in regard to the going ashore of the crew.
II.
Activities of the Maritime Council at Kobe.
The Maritime Council, composed of five representatives
of the shipping companies and fourteen representatives
of the members of crews, studied the question of control
of the illicit traffic in dangerous drugs on the 12th
of May 1936 and decided upon the following:
(a) Every shipping company shall be under obliga-
tion to make reports concerning crew members found
trafficking.
(b) A surveillance stricter than in the past
shall be carried out in ports.
(c) Rigorous inspection shall be carried out
on board while the ship is at sea.
(d) Every shipping company shall be under ob-
ligation constantly to keep drawing the attention of
captains to the necessity of efficient control (sic)
of the illicit traffic.
(e) The Maritime Council will present a petition
to the authorities of the Ministry of the Interior,
of the Ministry of Communications and of the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs, with a view to coordinating measures
to be taken for the control (sic) of the illicit traffic
in dangerous drugs.
(f)

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
(f) The question of illicit traffic in narcotic
drugs from Japan to the United States shall be studied
with special attention.
III.
Organization and measures undertaken by the authorities,
notably in the Prefectures of Kanagawa, Osaka, Hyogo
and Fukuoka (the ports of Yokohama, Osaka, Kobe and
Moji, Wakamatsu, Miike, Hakata, etc., are in these
prefectures).
(1) Organization.
Close cooperation is maintained by the Customs,
the Police and the Parquet, with a view to assuring
the control of the illicit traffic in narcotic drugs.
In the important ports, the Custonms authorities and
those of the police have organized a special corps
charged with the control of narcotic drugs.
(2) Measures taken by the authorities.
(a) Control of pharmacists.
Officers of the Customs, charged with the control
of narcotic drugs, carry out special surveillance of
the pharmacists in the ports.
(b) Surveillance of sailors in the ports.
A special surveillance is carried out over the
actions of suspected sailors in the ports and over
their relations with the people on shore. The Chinese
quarters of the important ports are subjected to
special surveillance.
(c)

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
(c) Black List and photographs.
Black Lists of smuglers and particularly of
sailors who have been mixed up in smuggling have been
established and utilized by the police, the customs
and the shipping companies. Whenever possible, photo-
graphs are attached to these lists.
(d) Inspection of bag age by the Customs.
A strict cooperation has been organized by the
Customs, the Police and the shipping companies concerning
strict surveillance of the baggage of passen ers and of
members of the crews.
(e) Inspection before departure.
Before the ships leave a port for the voyage across
the ocean, all members of the crew are lined up on the
deck, must respond to a roll call and must submit to
being searched. The members of the crew remain on the
deck under the surveillance of a squad of police while
another squad inspects the interior of the ship.
(f) Police on the ship.
Police officers, charged with the duty of inspec-
tion with a view to detecting the presence of dangerous
drugs are carried on ships destined to the Kwantung
Leased Territory or to China.

Subject: Proposed Visit of Metropolitan Police Official
to the United States.
Mr. Aoki, Section Chief in the Treaty Bureau of the
Japanese Foreign Office, called this morning to inquire
whether the American Government would have any objection
to an official of the Metropolitan Police proceeding to

in the narcotics control work centered in his Bureau and

put it, to be impressed with the sericusness with which our
officials work. I said that I was sure that our authorities
woulà welcome such a visit and that every facility would

NEW JAPANESE LKOISLATION
Mr. Taniguohi is actively engaged in drafting remodial
mareotio legislation. It is evidemt that immediate legislation
is being urged to control the traffie because the disgreee of
being regarded by other nations as the seat of an organized inter-
national 1llicit trafrie has penetrated to the Japanese public
whiek is demanding that as end be put to the seandal and that
this blight on her homor be removed.
Ne told Ir. Taniguohi that we had been urging the
Japenese Covernment for a number of years to increase the two-
month penalty so that it would have some deterrent effect. He
stated that the new law which was being drafted would provide
penalties of from three to five yearn for narsotic cffenses.
Japan signed the 1936 Convention to suppress the
illicit traffic in narootic drugs. This Convention obligates
governmemts to apply adequate penalties to narcotio violators.
The new legislatiom will also provide that if a Japanese
subject in the United States is sentenced to a term in the
penitentiary for mareotio violatiom and is subsequeatly deported,
he will, upom his arrival in Japan, be givem a sentence identi-
cal to that meted out to him in the United States. Mr. Tamigucki
asked that he not be quoted as the law was still in the couree of
preparatiem and he was not sure that his recommemdatioas weuld be

Shinji Taniguohi, Inspector of the Tokyo Metropolitan Police,
Tokyo, Japan, and is forwarded in compliance with the Bureau's
wire of the 5th instant.
Present at the conference with Inspector Taniguchi
were the following officers of the Treasury Department:

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.98)

hington, D. C. 2.
During the San Francisco conferenoe, Mr. Taniguchi
statedthat he is an Inspeotor of the Tokyo Metropolitan
Poliee in oharge of narootic investigations throughout Japan;
that the Japanese government did not realize the importance and
seriousness of the illegal narcotic trade between Japan and
the United States until the Tensaka seizure in Seattle, Wash-
ington, April 3, 1937; that the seriousness of the traffic
was brought to the attention of the entire nation of Japan
at that time due to the fact that Prinoe Chichibu, brother of
the Japanese Emperor, while en route to the coronation of
King George VI in England arrived in Seattle on the same
vessel which carried the narcotic drugs mentioned above, that
is, fifteen pounds of morphine concealed in soya sauce on the
Japanese M. S. "Heian Maru"; that such circumstances were
oonsidored a great disgrace by all Japanese subjects and that
his department had received strict urgent orders to immediately
take aotions to prevent the illegal shipment of narcotic drugs
from Japan to the United States and to investigate the Japanese
angles of the Tensaka case thoroughly. The Inspector continued
in great detail as to every move made in the investigation in
Japan in the above mentioned matter which disclosed a very
thorough, careful, successful investigation and evidenced the
incerity of the Japanese Police at least in this one case,
and involved such procedure as locatin the sweethearts and
women friends of suspect Nonaka, intercepting of telegrams and
letters and the placing in jails in Japan as well as jails in
China for long questioning of friends both male and fenale of
the suspect Nonaka. Also the questioning of all Japanese
arriving in that country from the Pacific Coast of the United
States. The investigation disclosed amont other things that
the cost of the morphine shipped to Seattle was 390 Yen per
pound and that two pounds of the contraband had been purchased
from the Sankyo Company of Kobe, Japan. The Inspector further
tated that the Japanese Government had called a conference of
the high ranking police officials and brought forth the serious-
ness of illegal narcotic exportations; that narcotic producing
factories in Japan could sell almost any amount to licensed
wholesalers who in turn could sell the licensed retailers; that a
serial mumber was allotted to each large sale and that number
oarried throughout in all transactions which afforded a basis
v
for oheoking but that, however, it was learned that the whole-
salers by falsifying records were furnishing the contraband
to illicit shippers to foreign countries; that severe action
was being taken at the present time by the Japanese police to
ourb such aotivity. Further, that the Government had no internal

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.98)
mas J. Gorman, Esq..
shington, D. 6. 3.
narootie problem due to the fact that there were practically
no Japanese addiots; that at the present time narcotio vio-
lations in Japan were considered a misdemeanor whioh called for
a maximum penalty of only ninety days imprisonment and a fine
of l00 Yen; that the non-payment of a fine resulted in a sen-
tence of one years' imprisonment regardless of the amount of the
fine; that a new law was being formulated to make narootio
violations a felony; that the Government was attempting to make
the maximum sentence a period of five years but whioh might
result in a maximum penalty of three years together with a fine
of 10,ooo Yen; that one of the purposes of his visit was to
thoroughly learn our laws so that he might be benefited in
assisting in formulating the new Japanese law; that another
purpose of his visit was to go into the Tensaka case as investi-
gated in the United States whioh might be of value to his investi-
gation in Japan; that his investigation had resulted in the
arrest of an associate in Japan of Tensaka's who assisted in the
shipment of narcotio drugs to Seattle. That tais individual.
named Nonaka, was now in jail in Japan and had been since May
awaiting trial; that another principal conspirator was involved and
was now being searched for by the Japanese Polioe; that his in-
vestigation of Nonaka had bee.. pursued from Japan to Tsing Tao,
Tientsin and Dairen, finally resulting in Nonaka's arrest at the
latter named oity; that as an outgrowth of the reoent orders to
oonoentrate on the illegal narcctio trade, 47 illicit dealers
had been arrested recently in Csake, Japan.
The Inspeotor's outline of the Nonaka-Tensaka inve t1-
gation conducted by himself was sutstantially the same or reported
through the Amerioan Eubass, at Tokyo under dat: of August 26,
1937, a oopy of whioh is attached to this report.
The Japanese official explaineu in detail his Cove:n-
mont's control of the manufacture and distribution of narootics
and the legal importation and exportation thereof, advising th:
no smoking opiun was allowed in Japan legally; that the narcutio
produoing faotories had to secure the approval of the Government
for the importation of raw opium and report the amount of manu-
faotured narootio drugs produced; further reporting to the Govern-
ment the sales of manufactured products to the wholesaler; that
each transaction bore a serial number; that the wholesaler must
1dentify his shipment by the same serial number and keep a reoord
for the Japanese Polioe of the dispensation thereof; that whole-
salers oould sell to eaoh other and to retailers; that physicians
must first seoure Polioe permission for the purohase from the
retailer of narootics.

Excellency's note No. 813 of October 28 expressing the
cordial appreciation of the United States Government for
the sending of Mr. Taniguchi of the Metropolitan Police
to the United States to investigate the smuggling of

report submitted by him sets forth that during his so-
journ in the United States he was accorded specially kind
treatment and assistance by the American authorities and as
a result he was able to be well informed in the matter and
to establish good relations with the American authorities.

to convey their thanks to the United States Government.

matter I have the honor to state that the Japanese Govern-
ment, in pursuit of its desire to suppress narcotics smuggling
on the Pacific, will endeavor to continue close rolations with
the United States Government with regard to the problem.

via Pacific routes, refers in nighly appreciative terms to
the assistance and courtesies which the officials of your
Bureau so generously accorded him during his stay in your
country. I wish to express to you, and through you, to
those officials my sincere thanks for the many kindnesses
thus done to Mr. Taniguchi.
I think it to be conducive to the cooperation between
Japan and the United States in preventing illicit traffic
in narcotic drugs that the competent officials of the two
countries should acquaint themselves with conditions on
each other's side of the Pacific. I assure you that, if
the United States sends officials to Japan for that purpose
on a suitable opportunity, we of this country will be very
glad to welcome them and place as many facilities as possible
at their disposal.
I am, Sir,
Yours respectfully,
(T. Mitani),
Mr. H.J.Anslinger,
Commissioner of
Director of the Bureau of
Narcotios,
Treaties and Conventions.
Treasury Department,
Washington, D.C.

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.99)
JAPAN AND THE DRUG TRADE
Sir,-Readers who may have thought that I was reckless in
my statements on Japan's promotion of the drug trade (in THe
NEW STATESMAN AND NATION of OctOber 9th) will have seen in
the Times' review of the report of the League of Nations
Opium Committee that the half was not told. How true to type
these meetings run! 'The Japanese delegate always expresses
astonishment and indignation; he cannot believe the statements
are true, and must make his own inquiries—and so another year
of uncontrolled drug-pushing is secured. After mentioning
the vast distribution of drugs in North China under Japanese
official protection, the Times says, " In Japan and Korca, on the
other hand, the laws against the cultivation and sale of narcoties are
strictly enforced." W'ho said so—the Japanese delegate? If the
extensive cultivation of opium poppies in Japan and Korea has
been stopped, it is only very recently, and because it attracts less
attention in Manchukuo. True, it was carefully controlled—up
to a point. But only the cream was skimmed off by the official
monopoly: the poppy-heads still containing half their opium
content were sold as " refuse " to the manufacturing chemists of
Osaka.
During the piping times of drug import, I was shown on one
occasion the import manifests of some ships discharging in Kobe,
and published the tigures in the Jupan Chronicle, pointing out
that these figures were greatly in excess of the returns published
by the Customs. I received an indignant letter from the Customs
saying that transhipment cargo never appeared in the returns.
Naturally the letter did not say whither the unrecorded drugs
were transhipped—they had certainly been consigned to Kobe.
But from that month narcotic drugs never appeared on the Kobe
import returns. Doubtless a cause of misunderstanding was thus
quietly removed.
Soon after the present trouble in China started I attended a
meeting of political intellectuals whose chief preoccupation was
to estimate what was the least gain that would satisfy Japan—for
the present. I did my best to get them interested in the fact that
where the Japanese army goes, narcotic drugs follow, but they
seemed to think it irrelevant. The Geneva opium reports are
yaluable, though rather belated, documents, and it seems a pity
hat they get no more publicity than a couple of newspaper
articles.
A. MORGAN YOUNG
Oxford.

I appreciate very mmuch your letter dated November 8, 1937.
It was both a privilege and a pleasure to meet Mr. Taniguchi
and to exchange information and viewpoints on our work which were
of mutual benefit. Personal contact of this type is by far the

is hoped that such a visit can e made in the course of another
year.

of Mr. Taniguchi's ability and to express the hope that we
shall be able to deal with him in the future in connection
with narcotic enforcement.

Supervising Customs Agent,

Bir:
You are respectfully referred to Bureau letter to us dated May 10,
having reference to Hajime Hoshi, Japanese drug manufacturer.
Mr. Hoshi arrived in San Francisco on May 23rd and remained here
until the afternoon of May 29th at which time he left for New York via
Los Angeles. As his baggage was handled by a Japanese transfer company
it was not felt advisable to question this company regarding the destina-
tion to which the baggage was checked. Mr. Hoshi had only a few handbags
and evidently nothing was checked through to destination. While we are
unable to give you any definite information as to Mr. Hoshi's activities,
he wae overheard to state that he intended to be in Los Angeles for only
a short time. He is then proceeding to New York from where he will embark
for South America.
Respectfully,
(Signed) D. W. Swift.
Customs Agent.
Respectfully forwarded,
Approved:
J. P. Sheehan, Acting
Supervising Customs Agent

Article 9. A person intending to import, or transport
within Japan, narcotics must file an application for a
pormit from the Minister of Home Affairs through the pre-
fectural governor of the territory in which his main business
office is located, and list the following items:
1. Name and quantity of articles.
2. Purpose of importing or transporting.
3. Name (title in case of a juridical person)
or trade name of the sender and the looation
of his business office.
4. The period of time during which the importing
or transporting will be carried out.
5. Method of shipping the goods.
6. Name of the port of importation or place to
which it is transported. (If sent by mail,
name of the post office).
In order to change items enumerated in the preceding
paragraph, after the above-mentioned permit has been obtained,
a new permit must be obtained for those items to be changed.
When the authorization of the first paragraph is granted
a pormit for importation or transportation and a certificate
of permission for importation or transportation will be pro-
vided by the authorities.
Artiole 10. A person desiring to export, or to ship to
amotmen point within Japan, narcotios must apply for the
amthorisation of the Minister of Home Affairs through the
prefeotural

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
repectural governor of the territory in whioh his main
basiness offiee is located, must state the following matters
and append the certificate of permission for importation
or transportation or certificate of permission for entering
goods into bonded warehouses issued by the proper authori-
ties at the destination of goods:
1. Name and quantity of goods.
2. Name (title in case of a juridical person)
or trade name of the receiver of the goods,
and the location of his business ofrice.
3. Period of time in which the exporting or ship-
ping will be carried out.
4. Method of shipping the goods.
5. Name of the port from which the goods are
exported or shipped.
In order to change items enumerated in the preceding
paragraph, after the permit has been obtained, a new permit
must be obtained for those items.
When the authorization of the first paragraph has been
granted, a permit for exportation or shipping and a duplicate
of that permit for exportation will be provided by the auth-
orities.
A duplioate of the permit for exportation prescribed
in the preceding paragraph must accompany the goods to be
shipped.
Artiole ll. The captain of a vessel carrying narcotics
from a foreign country to another may not carry such narootios
through Japan unless he presents to the competent customs
direetor at the time of entering a port, a duplicate of
a permit for exportation or of a certificate of transfer.

ties that Seilchi Amasaka, who was implieated in a seisure
ef merphine hydrechloride on the 8.s. Heian Maru on April 3,

of State is investigating the matter.

Exeerpt from "The Ohina Weekly Review"
of January 28, 1939.
Jananase Smach Opiun Rina in Janan
Aocording to Domel the Japanese police at Kanasawa,
broke up a gang of opium smugglers who were transporting
opium to China from Baghalien Island by way of Japan
and thenoe into Manchukuo and Tientsin. It was alleged
that Ohotaro Aeaco, leader of the mmuggling gang had
oleared profite amounting to a half million yem. Asada
and two associates were arrested. Nothing was said in
the roport as to why the Japanese were exporting opium
to Manohukuo whioh produces a large surplus of the drug
whioh 1s distributed throu hout Japanese-ocoupied are:s.
Also, it wes the first time that Saghalien (southern
half owned by Japan) was ever relerred to as a produoer
